# MODULE 12 — SESSION 12.3: Free Cash Flow — The Only Number That Doesn't Lie

> *Two companies each generate ₹1,000 crore of operating cash this year. The first hands ₹800 crore to its investors. The second has ₹0 left — every rupee got swallowed by new factories and a mountain of inventory. Same operating cash, opposite free cash. One of these businesses is a cash machine and the other is a treadmill, and only one number tells you which is which.*

---

## SESSION OVERVIEW

**What you'll learn:** What *free* cash flow actually means (the cash left after a business has paid to keep itself alive and growing), why it's far harder to fake than profit, and how to compute it. We'll separate the two flavours — **FCFF** (to the whole firm) and **FCFE** (to equity holders) — and derive the FCFF formula component by component, straight from the income statement and balance sheet. Then we expose the Indian manufacturing trap (high EBIT, near-zero cash), learn to *normalise* cash flows for one-offs and cycles, and prove that *how* a company reinvests its cash matters more than how much it generates.

**Why it matters:** Free cash flow is the "CF" in the DCF formula from Session 12.2. Get FCF wrong and every valuation downstream is wrong. It's also the single most reliable lie-detector in finance — a company can fake profits for years, but it cannot fake the balance in its bank account forever.

**Session difficulty:** ★★★★☆ *(This is the most mechanically demanding session so far — and the one that separates people who can actually build a model from people who can only talk about one.)*

**Damodaran insight of the session:** Cash flows are where stories meet reality. You can tell any growth story you like, but free cash flow is the toll the story has to pay — every rupee of growth demands cash for inventory, receivables, and capacity, and what's *left over* is what the business is truly worth. In Indian terms: Titan can tell a beautiful Tanishq growth story, but to grow it must keep buying gold — and that gold sits on shelves as inventory, quietly eating the very cash the story promised.

---

## NOTES (8 CARDS)

---

### CARD 1

> 🎣 **HOOK — Operating cash is the salary; free cash is the savings. Most students confuse the two.**

You already learned in Session 12.2 that *cash is a fact and profit is an opinion*. True — but there's a second, sneakier trap waiting, and it catches even people who've cleared that first hurdle. It's the difference between **operating cash flow** and **free cash flow**.

Operating cash flow is the cash a business generates from running its operations — the money that comes in the door from selling things, after paying suppliers and staff. Sounds great. But here's the catch: a business can't keep that cash. To stay alive and grow, it must *plough money back in* — build new stores, replace worn-out machines, stock up on inventory, extend credit to customers. Only what's left *after* all of that is genuinely available to investors. That leftover is **free cash flow** — the cash that's actually *free*.

Think of it like your salary. Your gross salary (operating cash) looks impressive on the offer letter. But after rent, EMIs, groceries, and the bare minimum you must spend just to keep your life running, what lands in your savings account is a much smaller number. *That* number — not your CTC — is what tells you how wealthy you're actually getting. Free cash flow is a company's "savings after unavoidable expenses."

This is why two companies with identical operating cash can be worlds apart. A capital-light software firm like a TCS keeps most of its operating cash as free cash — it doesn't need factories or inventory. A capital-heavy steel mill spends nearly *all* its operating cash just maintaining and feeding the beast, leaving almost nothing free. The first is a cash machine; the second is a treadmill that runs hard to stay in place.

Valuation — the DCF you built in Session 12.2 — runs *entirely* on free cash flow, never operating cash flow and never profit. Because the only cash that can ever reach a shareholder's pocket is the cash that's actually free.

**🧠 Remember:** Operating cash is what the business earns; free cash flow is what's left after it pays to keep itself alive and growing — and only the free part can ever reach investors, which is why every valuation runs on it.

---

### CARD 2

> 💡 **CONCEPT: FCFF vs FCFE — two cash flows, two audiences, two discount rates.**

Free cash flow comes in two flavours, and mixing them up is one of the most common — and most embarrassing — errors in an interview. The distinction is simple once you ask one question: *cash that's free for whom?*

**FCFF — Free Cash Flow to the Firm.** This is the cash available to *everyone* who funded the business — both lenders (debt) and shareholders (equity) — *before* any financing payments are made. It's the cash the business throws off purely from operations, with the financing question set aside entirely. Because it belongs to all capital providers, you discount it at the **WACC** (the blended cost of all capital — Session 12.4), and it gives you **Enterprise Value**. To get to equity, you then subtract net debt.

**FCFE — Free Cash Flow to Equity.** This is the cash available to *shareholders only*, *after* the lenders have been paid their interest and any debt principal. It's what's truly left for the owners. Because it belongs only to equity, you discount it at the **cost of equity (Ke)**, and it gives you **Equity Value directly** — no net-debt subtraction needed.

The bridge between them: **FCFE = FCFF − Interest × (1 − tax) + Net Borrowing.** In plain English, start with the firm's cash, pay the lenders their after-tax interest, and add back any new money the company borrowed (since fresh debt is cash that arrives for equity holders to use).

**When to use which?** In most equity research, analysts default to **FCFF discounted at WACC**, because it cleanly separates the operating business from how it's financed — useful when a company's debt levels are changing. **FCFE** shines when the capital structure is stable, and is the *only* sensible choice for **banks and NBFCs**, where debt (deposits, borrowings) *is* the raw material of the business and can't be separated out as "financing." That's why you'll value HDFC Bank on FCFE-style or excess-return models, never FCFF (Session 12.9). Done correctly and consistently, both paths land on the *same* equity value — they're two routes to the same destination.

**🧠 Remember:** FCFF is cash for all capital providers (discount at WACC → Enterprise Value); FCFE is cash for shareholders only (discount at cost of equity → Equity Value) — and you can't mix the cash flow with the wrong discount rate.

---

### CARD 3

> 🔍 **DEEP DIVE: Building the FCFF formula component by component — nothing memorised.**

Here is the formula every analyst must know cold:

> **FCFF = EBIT × (1 − tax rate) + D&A − Capex − ΔWorking Capital**

But don't memorise it — *build* it. Each term answers a specific question, and once you see the logic, you'll reconstruct it forever. We're starting from the income statement and ending with the cash that's genuinely free.

**Start with EBIT (Earnings Before Interest and Tax).** Why here and not net profit? Because FCFF is the cash for *all* capital providers, so we deliberately start *before* interest is paid out to lenders — we don't want financing decisions polluting the operating picture. EBIT is pure operating profit. *(Source: income statement, the operating-profit line.)*

**× (1 − tax rate) → this gives NOPAT.** The government takes its cut regardless, so we tax the operating profit. We tax EBIT *as if the company had no debt* (an "unlevered" tax), because we're isolating the operating business. EBIT × (1 − t) is called **NOPAT** — Net Operating Profit After Tax — the after-tax operating profit belonging to everyone. *(Tax rate ≈ 25% in India, or pull the effective rate from the P&L.)*

**+ D&A (add back Depreciation & Amortisation).** Depreciation was *subtracted* to calculate EBIT — but it's a **non-cash** charge. No rupee actually left the bank when the accountant "depreciated" a machine; it's just spreading an old purchase across years. So we add it back, because we want *cash*, not accounting profit. *(Source: cash flow statement / notes.)*

**− Capex (subtract Capital Expenditure).** Here's a *real* cash outflow that the income statement hides: the money actually spent on new plant, machinery, and stores. To keep operating and to grow, the business must spend this cash — so it's not free. We subtract it. *(Source: cash flow statement, investing section — "purchase of fixed assets.")*

**− ΔWorking Capital (subtract the increase in net working capital).** As a business grows, it locks cash into **inventory** (goods on shelves) and **receivables** (credit given to customers), partly offset by **payables** (credit taken from suppliers). When net working capital *rises*, cash is trapped — a cash outflow. When it *falls*, cash is released — a cash inflow. *(Source: changes across two consecutive balance sheets.)*

What's left — NOPAT, plus the non-cash add-back, minus the genuine cash spent on assets and working capital — is the cash the business actually generates and can hand to its financiers. That's FCFF. Every term earned its place; nothing was memorised.

**🧠 Remember:** FCFF = take operating profit, tax it, add back non-cash depreciation, then subtract the real cash the business must spend on capex and working capital to keep running — and every term is there for a reason you can explain.

---

### CARD 4

> 🌍 **REAL WORLD: Titan Company — a brilliant business that converts only part of its profit into cash.**

**Company:** Titan Company Ltd (Tanishq, CaratLane, watches, eyewear) · Sector: Consumer / Jewellery · **NSE: TITAN**

Titan is one of India's finest businesses — a powerful brand, a long growth runway, high returns. So you'd expect its free cash flow to roughly match its profit, right? Let's actually compute it and see. *(Figures below are approximate FY24-scale numbers, rounded for teaching — directionally accurate, not to-the-rupee.)*

**The inputs:**
- Revenue: ~₹51,000 crore
- EBIT: ~₹5,200 crore
- Reported Net Profit (PAT): ~₹3,500 crore
- D&A: ~₹900 crore
- Capex: ~₹800 crore
- Increase in working capital: ~₹2,000 crore *(mostly gold inventory)*
- Tax rate: ~25%

**The FCFF calculation, step by step:**

| Step | Item | ₹ crore | Running total |
|------|------|---------|---------------|
| 1 | EBIT | 5,200 | |
| 2 | × (1 − 0.25) → **NOPAT** | | 3,900 |
| 3 | + D&A (non-cash add-back) | +900 | 4,800 |
| 4 | − Capex | −800 | 4,000 |
| 5 | − Increase in Working Capital | −2,000 | **2,000** |

**FCFF ≈ ₹2,000 crore.**

Now compare: **reported profit ≈ ₹3,500 crore, but free cash flow ≈ ₹2,000 crore** — barely 60% of profit reached the "free" stage. Where did the rest go? Look at Step 5: **₹2,000 crore got locked into additional inventory** — and for a jeweller, inventory means *gold sitting in showcases*. To sell more jewellery, Titan must *stock* more jewellery, and gold is expensive. So a huge slice of its profit doesn't reach investors; it sits on shelves, funding growth.

**The most important nuance — this is "good" cash consumption.** Unlike a struggling company whose cash vanishes into losses, Titan's cash is going into *productive, high-return* inventory that will be sold at a healthy margin. It's growth investment, not value destruction. So the lesson isn't "Titan is a bad business" — it's the opposite: *even a wonderful business can have FCF well below profit, and that's fine as long as the reinvestment earns strong returns* (the exact point of Card 7). The mistake would be to value Titan on its ₹3,500 crore profit as though all of it were distributable cash. It isn't.

**The lesson for every working-capital-heavy business** (jewellers, distributors, traders, auto-component makers): profit and free cash flow diverge, and the gap lives in the working-capital line. Always check how much of reported profit is being eaten by inventory and receivables before you trust the profit number as "cash."

**🧠 Remember:** Titan earns ~₹3,500 cr profit but generates only ~₹2,000 cr of free cash because growth forces it to lock cash into gold inventory — proof that even a great business converts only part of its profit to cash, and you must value the *cash*, not the profit.

---

### CARD 5

> ⚠️ **THE MISTAKE: The Indian manufacturing trap — reading high EBIT as if it were cash.**

A student opens a steel company's results, sees a fat EBIT of ₹5,000 crore in a good year, glances at the low P/E, and concludes: *"Strong profits, cheap stock — obvious buy."* This is the single most expensive mistake in valuing Indian capital-intensive businesses, and it has wrecked more portfolios than almost any other error.

**Why it's so easy to make:** EBIT and profit are the headline numbers everyone quotes, and in an *up-cycle* a steel or cement company genuinely posts huge operating profits. The trap is invisible because the cash drain hides in two places the income statement doesn't show you: the capex line and the working-capital line.

**Why it's wrong — watch the cash vanish.** Let's run the same FCFF math on a steel producer in a strong year:

| Item | ₹ crore |
|------|---------|
| EBIT | 5,000 |
| NOPAT (× 0.75) | 3,750 |
| + D&A | +3,000 |
| − Capex | −5,000 |
| − ΔWorking Capital | −2,000 |
| **FCFF** | **−250** |

A company with **₹5,000 crore of EBIT generated *negative* free cash flow.** How? Two structural facts of heavy manufacturing: (1) **capex is enormous** — steel plants are insatiable, constantly needing replacement and expansion, so capex often *equals or exceeds* depreciation; and (2) **working capital balloons** — mountains of iron ore, coal, and finished steel inventory, plus receivables from customers, swallow cash, especially when volumes rise in an up-cycle. The "profit" is real on paper but it never becomes free cash — it gets reincarnated as more plant and more inventory. This is the lived reality of companies like Tata Steel, JSW Steel, and SAIL across the cycle: positive EBIT, frequently negative or volatile FCF.

**The correct approach:** never value a capital-intensive business on EBIT or profit alone — go straight to free cash flow, and pay special attention to capex intensity (is capex chronically above depreciation?) and the working-capital cycle. For cyclicals, also normalise across the cycle (Card 6), because a peak-year FCF flatters and a trough-year FCF terrifies.

**What the interviewer thinks:** When a candidate says "steel looks cheap on P/E, so it's a buy," the interviewer hears *"doesn't understand capital intensity — will get destroyed by cyclicals."* When a candidate says "the EBIT looks strong but I'd check whether capex and working capital leave any actual free cash," the interviewer thinks *"this one has been burned by a balance sheet before — good."*

**🧠 Remember:** A capital-intensive business can post huge EBIT and still bleed free cash, because heavy capex and ballooning working capital silently consume the profit — so for manufacturers and cyclicals, never trust EBIT as a proxy for cash.

---

### CARD 6

> 💡 **CONCEPT: Normalisation — turning one noisy year into sustainable cash.**

The FCF you feed into a DCF is meant to represent the company's *sustainable, recurring* cash generation — what it can produce year after year in normal conditions. But any single year is noisy, distorted by one-offs and cycles. Cleaning up that noise is called **normalisation**, and skipping it is how analysts accidentally value a fluke.

**1. Strip out one-time items.** A single year's cash flow can be thrown off by non-recurring events: a one-off litigation settlement, a gain from selling a building, an insurance payout, a restructuring charge, a tax refund. None of these will repeat, so leaving them in either flatters or depresses your "normal" FCF. The fix: identify and remove them, valuing only the *recurring* cash engine. If Titan sold a piece of land for ₹300 crore this year, that ₹300 crore is not part of next year's jewellery cash flow.

**2. Separate maintenance capex from growth capex.** Capex has two jobs: *maintenance* capex just keeps existing capacity running (replacing worn-out machines), while *growth* capex builds *new* capacity to expand. This distinction is huge for valuation — a company's "steady-state" free cash flow really only needs to cover maintenance capex; growth capex is discretionary investment that *should* generate future returns. Companies almost never disclose the split, so analysts estimate it (a common rough proxy: maintenance capex ≈ depreciation). A business that *looks* like it has poor FCF may simply be spending heavily on *growth* — very different from one bleeding cash just to stand still.

**3. Normalise for the cycle.** For cyclical businesses (steel, cement, autos, chemicals), a single year is dangerously misleading — you might be catching a peak or a trough. Valuing a steel company on peak-cycle margins and then assuming those margins last *forever* in the terminal value massively overvalues it. The fix: use **mid-cycle** or through-the-cycle average margins to estimate normalised FCF, not whatever this particular year happened to deliver.

The unifying idea: the DCF asks "what can this business sustainably generate?" — so your job is to look through the noise of any one year to the durable cash engine underneath. An analyst who feeds a raw, un-normalised, peak-cycle, one-off-inflated number into a perpetuity is building a beautiful model on a lie.

**🧠 Remember:** Before using FCF in a DCF, normalise it — strip one-time items, separate maintenance from growth capex, and use mid-cycle figures for cyclicals — because you're valuing the *sustainable* cash engine, not one noisy year.

---

### CARD 7

> 📊 **VISUAL: Why reinvesting at 8% destroys value even when your business earns 20%.**

*[Plain English description — what the student should imagine]*

The deepest truth about free cash flow isn't *how much* a company generates — it's what it does with the cash it *keeps*. Picture two companies, each reinvesting ₹100 of cash, both facing a 12% cost of capital (WACC). Company A reinvests at a 20% return; Company B at 8%. Draw two paths. **Company A:** ₹100 invested earns ₹20 every year forever; the present value of that ₹20-per-year stream at 12% is ₹167 — so spending ₹100 *created* ₹167 of value, a gain of **+₹67**. **Company B:** ₹100 invested earns only ₹8 a year forever; the present value at 12% is just ₹67 — so spending ₹100 produced something worth only ₹67, a loss of **−₹33**. Same ₹100 reinvested. One *creates* value; the other *destroys* it — purely because of the return earned versus the cost of capital. A horizontal line at "WACC = 12%" marks the dividing line: reinvest above it and you create value; below it and growth actively makes shareholders poorer.

**AI Image Generation Prompt:**
> Create a widescreen 16:9 financial infographic, dark cosmic aesthetic — deep navy background (#02073D) with a faint starfield. Title at top in clean white sans-serif: "GROWTH ONLY CREATES VALUE ABOVE YOUR COST OF CAPITAL". Centre: a horizontal dashed reference line spanning the width labelled in #F5ECFF lavender "COST OF CAPITAL (WACC) = 12%". ABOVE the line, a glowing #246BFD blue panel labelled "COMPANY A — reinvests at 20%": show "₹100 invested → ₹20/year forever → worth ₹167 today" with a large #6A5CFF purple callout "VALUE CREATED +₹67". BELOW the line, a dimmer #FF6AC1 pink panel labelled "COMPANY B — reinvests at 8%": show "₹100 invested → ₹8/year forever → worth ₹67 today" with a red-tinged callout "VALUE DESTROYED −₹33". Between them, a small icon of a single ₹100 note splitting into two arrows, one going up (green tint) and one going down (red tint), emphasising SAME cash, opposite outcomes. Captions in small JetBrains-Mono-style monospace. Bottom caption in lavender: "It's not how much cash you generate — it's the return you earn on what you keep." Style: clean, modern, high-contrast, generous spacing, Bricolage-Grotesque-style headings, no clutter.

**Why this visual works:** It makes the most counter-intuitive idea in finance physical — that a "growing" company can be *destroying* value — by putting the WACC line in the middle and showing the same ₹100 producing opposite results on either side of it. A student who sees this will never again assume that high free cash flow or fast growth is automatically good; they'll ask the real question: *what return does the company earn on the cash it reinvests?*

**🧠 Remember:** Growth creates value only when the return on reinvested cash beats the cost of capital — a company earning 20% but reinvesting new cash at 8% (below a 12% WACC) destroys value as it grows, so always judge the *quality* of reinvestment, not just the quantity of cash.

---

### CARD 8

> 📝 **SESSION SUMMARY: What You Now Know**

```
🎯 The Big Idea:
Free cash flow is the cash a business generates AFTER paying to keep itself
alive and growing — the only cash that can ever reach an investor, the hardest
number to fake, and the fuel that runs every DCF.

✅ 5 Things You Now Know:
1. Operating cash ≠ free cash. Free cash is what's left after capex and working
   capital — a business's "savings after unavoidable expenses."
2. FCFF (cash to the whole firm, discount at WACC → Enterprise Value) vs FCFE
   (cash to equity only, discount at cost of equity → Equity Value). Banks/NBFCs
   use FCFE; don't mix a cash flow with the wrong discount rate.
3. FCFF = EBIT × (1 − tax) + D&A − Capex − ΔWorking Capital. Every term is there
   for a reason you can explain — it's a derivation, not a spell.
4. High EBIT ≠ high cash. Capital-intensive businesses (steel, cement) can post
   huge profit and NEGATIVE free cash because capex and working capital eat it all.
5. It's not how much cash you generate, it's the return on what you reinvest —
   growth below your cost of capital DESTROYS value.

⚡ The Analyst Move:
A textbook student takes reported profit as "the cash." A working analyst rebuilds
FCF from scratch, normalises out the one-offs and the cycle, checks how much profit
is being eaten by working capital — and then asks whether the retained cash is being
reinvested ABOVE the cost of capital.

🔗 Connects to:
• Session 12.2 (DCF) — FCF is the "CF" you discount; this session built it properly.
• Session 12.4 (WACC) — FCFF is discounted at WACC, the rate we derive next.
• Session 12.9 (Sector Valuation) — why banks/NBFCs use FCFE-style models, not FCFF.

💬 If someone asks you "what is free cash flow?" at an interview, say:
"Free cash flow is the cash a business generates after it's paid for everything it
needs to keep operating and growing — capex and working capital. It's the only cash
that can actually reach investors, and it's much harder to manipulate than profit,
which is why a DCF runs on it. I'd compute FCFF as EBIT after tax, plus non-cash
depreciation, minus capex and the change in working capital — and I'd be careful to
normalise for one-time items and where the company is in its cycle, because I'm after
sustainable cash, not one noisy year."
```

---
---

## QUIZ (5 QUESTIONS)

---

**Q1. Free Cash Flow to the Firm (FCFF) represents cash available to whom, and at what rate is it discounted?**

A) Equity holders only; discounted at the cost of equity
B) All capital providers (debt and equity); discounted at the WACC
C) The government; discounted at the risk-free rate
D) Suppliers; discounted at the inflation rate

**✅ Answer: B**

**Explanation:** FCFF is the cash the business generates for *all* its financiers — both lenders and shareholders — before any financing payments, so it's discounted at the WACC (the blended cost of all capital) and yields Enterprise Value. Option A describes FCFE, which is discounted at the cost of equity and gives equity value directly. C and D misidentify the audience entirely. Matching the right cash flow to the right discount rate is the core discipline here.

**Difficulty:** Easy

---

**Q2. A company has EBIT of ₹1,000 crore, a tax rate of 25%, D&A of ₹200 crore, capex of ₹300 crore, and an increase in working capital of ₹150 crore. What is its FCFF?**

A) ₹500 crore
B) ₹750 crore
C) ₹600 crore
D) ₹950 crore

**✅ Answer: A**

**Explanation:** FCFF = EBIT × (1 − tax) + D&A − Capex − ΔWC = 1,000 × 0.75 + 200 − 300 − 150 = 750 + 200 − 300 − 150 = ₹500 crore. Option B (₹750) stops at NOPAT, forgetting the D&A add-back and the capex/working-capital subtractions. Option D (₹950) forgets to subtract capex and working capital. The discipline is to apply *every* term, not stop early.

**Difficulty:** Medium

---

**Q3. Why do analysts value banks and NBFCs (like HDFC Bank) using FCFE-style models rather than FCFF?**

A) Because banks have no cash flows
B) Because for a bank, debt (deposits and borrowings) is the raw material of the business and can't be cleanly separated from "financing," so FCFF is meaningless
C) Because banks are always overvalued
D) Because FCFE is easier to calculate for every company

**✅ Answer: B**

**Explanation:** FCFF deliberately strips out financing to isolate the operating business — but for a bank, *financing IS the operating business*: it takes deposits (debt) and lends them out, so interest is an operating item, not a side issue. You can't separate the two, which makes FCFF nonsensical, so banks are valued on FCFE-style or excess-return/P-B frameworks. Option A is false (banks have substantial cash flows), C is an unrelated value judgment, and D is wrong — FCFE isn't universally easier and isn't the default for most non-financial firms.

**Difficulty:** Medium

---

**Q4. Titan (NSE: TITAN) earns roughly ₹3,500 crore in net profit but generates only about ₹2,000 crore of free cash flow. What is the dominant reason for the gap?**

A) Titan is committing accounting fraud
B) Large cash is being locked into additional inventory (gold) to fund jewellery growth — an increase in working capital
C) Titan pays an unusually high tax rate
D) Titan has enormous interest payments on debt

**✅ Answer: B**

**Explanation:** To grow jewellery sales, Titan must stock more jewellery, and gold inventory is expensive — so a large chunk of profit gets trapped in the working-capital line rather than reaching investors as free cash. Crucially, this is *productive* growth investment, not fraud (A) — the cash is funding high-return inventory. Titan's tax rate is normal (C), and it is a low-debt company, so interest is small (D). The gap between profit and cash for working-capital-heavy businesses almost always lives in inventory and receivables.

**Difficulty:** Hard

---

**Q5. A steel company earns 20% on its existing capital but can only reinvest new cash at an 8% return, while its cost of capital (WACC) is 12%. What happens when it reinvests that cash to grow?**

A) It creates value, because 20% is a high return
B) It destroys value, because the *incremental* return (8%) is below the cost of capital (12%)
C) Value is unaffected, because growth is always neutral
D) It creates value only if it borrows to fund the growth

**✅ Answer: B**

**Explanation:** Value creation depends on the return on the *newly reinvested* cash, not the company's historical headline return. Reinvesting at 8% when capital costs 12% means every rupee deployed returns less than it costs — so growth actively makes shareholders poorer, regardless of the impressive 20% legacy ROCE. Option A is the classic trap of anchoring to the headline return; C ignores the cost-of-capital test entirely; and D is false — borrowing doesn't rescue a sub-cost-of-capital investment, it just changes who bears the loss.

**Difficulty:** Hard

---
---

## INTERVIEW QUESTIONS (3 QUESTIONS)

---

**IQ1. "What's the difference between FCFF and FCFE?"**

*Difficulty:* Junior Analyst
*Type:* Technical
*Firm type likely to ask:* All

**💬 Strong answer:**
"FCFF is free cash flow to the firm — the cash available to all capital providers, both debt and equity, before any financing payments. I'd discount it at the WACC to get Enterprise Value, then subtract net debt to reach equity value. FCFE is free cash flow to equity — the cash left for shareholders only, after lenders have been paid interest and any principal. I'd discount that at the cost of equity to get equity value directly. The bridge is FCFE equals FCFF minus after-tax interest plus net new borrowing. In practice I'd default to FCFF discounted at WACC for most companies because it separates operations from financing cleanly, but I'd use FCFE for businesses with stable capital structures — and I'd *always* use an FCFE-style approach for banks and NBFCs, where debt is the raw material of the business and can't be stripped out as financing."

**🚫 Weak answer trap:** Discounting FCFF at the cost of equity, or FCFE at WACC — mixing the cash flow with the wrong discount rate is an instant red flag that you don't actually understand what each represents.

**✅ What this question tests:** Whether you understand *who* each cash flow belongs to, which dictates the discount rate and the output. It's a foundational technical question that separates people who memorised formulas from people who understand them.

---

**IQ2. "Walk me from EBIT to free cash flow to the firm."**

*Difficulty:* Junior Analyst
*Type:* Technical
*Firm type likely to ask:* All

**💬 Strong answer:**
"I'd start with EBIT, the operating profit, because FCFF is pre-financing so I want to begin before interest. First I tax it — EBIT times one minus the tax rate — to get NOPAT, the after-tax operating profit available to everyone, taxed as if there were no debt. Then I add back depreciation and amortisation, because those were subtracted to get EBIT but no cash actually left the business — they're non-cash. Next I subtract capex, which is real cash spent on plant and equipment that the income statement doesn't capture. Finally I subtract the increase in working capital, because as the business grows it locks cash into inventory and receivables. What's left — NOPAT plus the non-cash add-back, minus the genuine cash spent on assets and working capital — is the free cash the business throws off for its financiers. If working capital *falls* in a year, that's a cash inflow and it gets added instead."

**🚫 Weak answer trap:** Forgetting to subtract the change in working capital (the most commonly dropped term), or starting from net profit instead of EBIT without adjusting for interest — both reveal a half-understood derivation.

**✅ What this question tests:** Whether you can reconstruct the FCFF build live and *explain why* each adjustment exists, not just recite the formula. This is bread-and-butter for any modelling role.

---

**IQ3. "A company's reported profits are growing nicely, but its free cash flow has been negative for three straight years. Walk me through how you'd decide whether that's a problem."**

*Difficulty:* Senior Analyst
*Type:* Judgment
*Firm type likely to ask:* Buy-side / Sell-side

**💬 Strong answer:**
"I wouldn't panic, but I'd investigate hard, because the answer depends entirely on *why* the cash is going out. First I'd decompose the gap — is the negative FCF driven by capex or by working capital? If it's heavy *growth* capex, the business may be profitably expanding, like a retailer rolling out stores, and that's potentially great as long as those new investments earn a return above the cost of capital. If it's a ballooning working-capital cycle — receivables stretching out, inventory piling up — I'd be more worried, because that can signal channel-stuffing, weak collections, or demand problems, and it's a classic forensic red flag. I'd also check whether this is a cyclical business near a peak, where I'd normalise across the cycle. The decisive question is the quality of reinvestment: cash going into high-return growth is fine, but cash disappearing into a structurally cash-hungry business that earns below its cost of capital is value destruction dressed up as growth. So I'd separate 'investing for the future' from 'running to stand still' before forming a view."

**🚫 Weak answer trap:** Either "negative FCF means it's a bad company, avoid" or "profits are growing so it's fine" — both miss the point. The mature answer distinguishes *good* cash consumption (high-return growth) from *bad* (a cash-trap business or a working-capital red flag).

**✅ What this question tests:** Whether you can reason about *why* profit and cash diverge and judge it case by case — connecting the manufacturing trap, normalisation, and capital-allocation quality into a single coherent judgment. This is exactly the analytical maturity buy-side and sell-side roles demand.

---

*End of Session 12.3 — Free Cash Flow: The Only Number That Doesn't Lie*
*Module 12 — Valuation in Equity Research | Zero to Analyst: The Complete Equity Research Career Program by Valuevade*


<div style="page-break-after: always;"></div>

# MODULE 12 — SESSION 12.4: WACC — The Cost of Feeding a Company's Hunger

> *Most founders believe raising equity is "free money" — after all, you don't pay interest on it. This belief has quietly cost Indian entrepreneurs fortunes. Equity is in fact the* most *expensive money a company can raise — pricier than any bank loan. Understand why, and you understand the single number that decides whether a company is creating value or destroying it: the WACC.*

---

## SESSION OVERVIEW

**What you'll learn:** What a company's "cost of capital" actually is, and why it's the hurdle every rupee of investment must clear. We'll tell the two-creditors story, derive the WACC formula from first principles, and break down its hardest piece — the **cost of equity** via CAPM (risk-free rate, beta, equity risk premium) — using real Indian numbers. We'll wrestle with the Indian-specific debates (what's truly "risk-free" when the G-Sec yields 7%? why is raw NSE beta unreliable?), compute WACC for Asian Paints end to end, and finish with the test every analyst must run: is the company earning more than its capital costs, or less?

**Why it matters:** WACC is the "r" in the DCF formula from Session 12.2 — the rate that does *all* the discounting. A one-percentage-point error in WACC can swing a valuation by well over 10% (you saw this in the kirana models). Worse, the WACC vs ROCE comparison is the truest test of whether a business creates or destroys value — and most students never run it.

**Session difficulty:** ★★★★☆ *(Conceptually rich, with several genuinely contested judgment calls — this is where valuation stops being arithmetic and becomes craft.)*

**Damodaran insight of the session:** The cost of capital is not a number you look up — it's the price the market charges a company for the risk of its business, and it shifts with that risk. Lowering your WACC by assumption to make a stock look cheap is the oldest self-deception in valuation. In Indian terms: if you're discounting a volatile small-cap at the same 11% you'd use for HDFC Bank, you're not being optimistic — you're being dishonest about risk.

---

## NOTES (8 CARDS)

---

### CARD 1

> 🎣 **HOOK — Why equity is the most expensive money a company can raise.**

Ask a first-year B.Com student which is cheaper: borrowing ₹100 crore from a bank, or raising ₹100 crore by selling shares. Almost everyone says equity — "you don't pay interest on shares, so it's free." This is one of the most expensive misconceptions in all of finance, and founders who believe it give away enormous value.

Here's the truth: **equity is more expensive than debt — always.** To see why, ask one question: *who gets paid last?* When a company's cash comes in, there's a strict pecking order. Suppliers get paid. Employees get paid. The government takes its tax. Lenders get their interest. And whatever is left — *if* anything is left — goes to the equity shareholders. Shareholders are the **residual claimants**: last in line, first to lose, no guaranteed return.

Because they bear the most risk, equity investors *demand* the highest return. A bank lending to Asian Paints might accept 8% because it's first in line and can sue if unpaid. But an equity investor in the *same* company demands 12–14% — because they could lose everything if the business fails, and they want compensation for that risk. Debt has a contract; equity has a prayer. Risk and required return move together, so the riskiest capital is the most expensive.

This flips the founder's intuition. "Free" equity isn't free — its cost is just *invisible*, paid not in interest but in the returns shareholders expect (and in the ownership you dilute). A founder who raises equity at a 14% implied cost to avoid an 8% loan has chosen the *more* expensive option without realising it.

Now zoom out. A company funds itself with a *mix* of these two kinds of money — cheap debt and expensive equity — each demanding its own return. The blended cost of all of it is the **Weighted Average Cost of Capital (WACC)**: the single percentage that captures what it costs to "feed" the entire company. It's the hurdle rate, the discount rate, the bar every investment must clear. This session is about computing it honestly.

**🧠 Remember:** Equity is the most expensive capital because shareholders are paid last and bear the most risk — and WACC is the blended cost of a company's cheap debt and expensive equity, the hurdle every investment must clear.

---

### CARD 2

> 🔍 **DEEP DIVE: The WACC formula, derived — and why debt gets a tax discount.**

The formula looks intimidating but it's pure common sense once you build it:

> **WACC = (E/V) × Ke + (D/V) × Kd × (1 − tax rate)**

A company is fed by two kinds of money, so its overall cost of capital is just the **average of the two costs, weighted by how much of each it uses.** Think of a thali with two dishes priced differently — your average cost per meal depends on the proportions. Let's define every term.

**The weights — E/V and D/V.** Let **E** = the market value of equity (the market cap), **D** = the market value of debt, and **V = E + D** (total capital). Then **E/V** is the fraction of the company funded by equity, and **D/V** the fraction funded by debt. These weights *must* use **market values, not book values** — a point so important it gets its own card (Card 5).

**The costs — Ke and Kd.** **Ke** is the cost of equity (the return shareholders demand — we compute it via CAPM in Card 3). **Kd** is the *pre-tax* cost of debt (the interest rate the company pays lenders).

**The (1 − tax rate) — debt's secret discount.** Here's the elegant part. Interest is **tax-deductible**. When a company pays ₹100 of interest, it reduces its taxable profit by ₹100 — saving ₹25 in tax (at a 25% rate). So that ₹100 of interest *really* costs the company only ₹75. This is the **tax shield**, and it's why we multiply the cost of debt by (1 − t): an 8% loan has an effective post-tax cost of just 8% × 0.75 = **6%**. Crucially, equity gets *no* such shield — dividends aren't tax-deductible — which makes debt even cheaper relative to equity than the headline rates suggest.

**How to estimate Kd in practice.** Two ways: (1) divide the **interest expense from the P&L by total debt** to get the average borrowing rate, or (2) take the **risk-free rate plus a credit spread** based on the company's rating — a AAA-rated firm like Asian Paints borrows at roughly the G-Sec yield plus ~1%, so ~8%.

Put it together and WACC is simply: *the cost of equity times the equity weight, plus the after-tax cost of debt times the debt weight.* Every term earned its place.

**🧠 Remember:** WACC = (equity weight × cost of equity) + (debt weight × after-tax cost of debt) — and debt is cheaper than it looks because interest is tax-deductible, creating a tax shield equity never gets.

---

### CARD 3

> 💡 **CONCEPT: Cost of equity via CAPM — pricing the risk shareholders bear.**

The hardest input in WACC is the cost of equity, because — unlike a loan — there's no contract stating what shareholders are "owed." So how do we put a number on the return equity investors *demand*? The standard tool is the **Capital Asset Pricing Model (CAPM)**:

> **Ke = Rf + β × ERP**

Read it as a recipe: *start with what you'd earn risk-free, then add a premium for taking equity risk, scaled by how risky this particular stock is.* Three ingredients:

**1. Rf — the risk-free rate.** The return you can earn with (almost) zero risk — in India, the 10-year government bond (G-Sec) yield, around **7%**. This is the floor. No rational investor accepts *less* than the risk-free rate to take *more* risk, so every cost of equity starts here.

**2. ERP — the equity risk premium.** The *extra* return investors demand for bearing the risk of equities over the safe asset. If equities are expected to return ~13.5% and the risk-free rate is 7%, the ERP is ~6.5%. It's the reward for stomaching market volatility instead of parking money in bonds. In India, this sits around **6.5–7.5%**.

**3. β (beta) — the stock's risk *relative to the market*.** This scales the premium to the specific stock. **β = 1** means the stock moves in line with the market (it gets the full ERP). **β > 1** means it's *more* volatile than the market — riskier, so investors demand *more* than the full premium (a β of 1.3 means 1.3× the ERP). **β < 1** means it's *less* volatile — a defensive stock like a consumer staple, which demands *less* premium. Beta is the dial that tunes the market's risk premium up or down for this particular company.

So for a defensive stock with β = 0.8, in India: Ke = 7% + 0.8 × 6.5% = 7% + 5.2% = **12.2%**. For an aggressive small-cap with β = 1.4: Ke = 7% + 1.4 × 6.5% = **16.1%**. The riskier the stock, the higher its cost of equity — exactly as it should be.

**The deepest point:** CAPM says investors are only rewarded for **market risk** (risk you can't diversify away), captured by beta — not for company-specific risk, which a diversified investor can eliminate by holding many stocks. That's why a single, undiversifiable factor (beta) sets the price of risk.

**🧠 Remember:** Cost of equity = risk-free rate + beta × equity risk premium — you start with the safe return, add a premium for equity risk, and scale it by beta, the stock's volatility relative to the market.

---

### CARD 4

> 🔍 **DEEP DIVE: The three CAPM inputs in India — where the textbook gets messy.**

CAPM is clean in theory. Estimating its inputs *in the Indian market* is where judgment enters — and where good analysts separate from formula-pluggers. Let's pin down each.

**Rf — what's truly "risk-free" in India?** The standard choice is the **10-year G-Sec yield (~7%)**. But here's the uncomfortable nuance: it isn't *perfectly* risk-free. India's sovereign rating is around BBB– (not AAA), so the G-Sec embeds a small **sovereign default premium**, and the yield also carries inflation and reinvestment risk. Damodaran's purist approach is to *strip out* India's default spread (estimated from the country's credit rating or CDS) to get a "clean" risk-free rate. In practice, most Indian analysts simply use the 10-year G-Sec yield (~7%) directly as Rf — it's defensible, transparent, and what the market itself references. Just know *why* it's an approximation.

**ERP — India's equity risk premium.** This is the reward for holding Indian equities over Indian G-Secs, and it's **higher than in developed markets** because emerging markets carry more risk. Damodaran builds India's ERP as the *mature-market premium* (the US base, ~4.5–5%) *plus* an **India country risk premium** derived from the default spread, scaled for equity volatility — which has historically put India's ERP in roughly the **7–8%** range, though it moves year to year. Many practitioners use a working figure of **~6.5–7%**. The exact number is contestable; what matters is you *justify* it rather than pluck it from air.

**β — why raw NSE beta is unreliable, and what to do instead.** The obvious move is to regress a stock's returns against the Nifty to get a "raw" beta. *Don't trust it blindly.* Regression betas in India are **noisy and unstable** — they swing wildly depending on the time period and frequency you choose, they're distorted by stock-specific events and thin trading, and the statistical fit (R²) for individual stocks is often weak. The professional fix is a **bottom-up (industry) beta**: (1) take a group of comparable companies in the same business, (2) *unlever* each peer's beta to strip out the effect of its specific debt load, (3) average those unlevered "business risk" betas, then (4) *relever* the average for *your* company's own debt-to-equity ratio. The result is far more stable, reflects the *fundamental* risk of the business rather than the noise of one stock's price chart, and is the approach Damodaran champions.

The meta-lesson: every CAPM input in India involves a defensible *choice*, not a lookup. Two honest analysts can land on WACCs of 11.5% and 13% for the same company — and both can be right, *given their stated assumptions*. Show your working.

**🧠 Remember:** In India, use the ~7% G-Sec as Rf (knowing it's not perfectly risk-free), a ~6.5–8% equity risk premium you can justify, and a bottom-up peer beta rather than the noisy raw NSE regression beta — every input is a defensible judgment, not a lookup.

---

### CARD 5

> ⚠️ **THE MISTAKE: Using book-value weights instead of market-value weights.**

This is the single most common WACC error, and it's lethal because the formula still "works" — it just quietly produces the wrong number. The mistake: computing the E/V and D/V weights using the **book values** of equity and debt from the balance sheet, instead of their **market values**.

**Why it's so easy to make:** the balance sheet is right there, with neat figures for "equity" (share capital + reserves) and "borrowings." It feels natural to use them. And for *debt*, book value is usually a fine proxy for market value (loans sit near par). The fatal problem is on the *equity* side.

**Why it's wrong — and how badly it distorts.** Book equity is a *historical accounting* figure — shares issued at old prices plus accumulated retained earnings. For any good company, it's a tiny fraction of what the equity is actually *worth* today (the market cap). Take Asian Paints: its **book equity (net worth) is roughly ₹14,000 crore, but its market capitalisation is around ₹2,70,000 crore** — nearly 20 times larger. Now watch what book-value weights do to the WACC. With debt of ~₹2,500 crore:
- **Market-value weights (correct):** D/V = 2,500 / (2,70,000 + 2,500) ≈ **0.9%**. The company is ~99% equity-funded.
- **Book-value weights (wrong):** D/V = 2,500 / (14,000 + 2,500) ≈ **15.2%**. The company *looks* ~15% debt-funded.

The book-value version *massively overstates* the debt proportion. Since debt is the *cheaper* capital, overweighting it drags the calculated WACC artificially **downward** — and a lower WACC inflates the DCF value. You'd be **overvaluing the company** purely through a weighting error, with no other mistake in the model.

**The correct approach:** always use the **market value of equity** (= market cap = share price × shares outstanding) and the market value of debt (book value of debt is an acceptable proxy unless it's traded at a notable discount/premium). WACC measures what investors demand *today*, so the weights must reflect *today's* values.

**What the interviewer thinks:** a candidate who uses book-value weights signals *"doesn't understand that WACC reflects current required returns — will systematically misvalue every quality company."* A candidate who says "I'd use market cap for the equity weight, not book equity" signals real understanding.

**🧠 Remember:** Always weight WACC by *market* values, not book — book equity can be a fraction of market cap (Asian Paints: ₹14,000 cr book vs ₹2,70,000 cr market), and using it overstates the debt weight, understates WACC, and overvalues the company.

---

### CARD 6

> 🌍 **REAL WORLD: Computing WACC for Asian Paints, step by step.**

**Company:** Asian Paints Ltd · Sector: Consumer / Paints · **NSE: ASIANPAINT**

Let's put every piece together on a real company. Asian Paints is a high-quality, defensive, **nearly debt-free** business — which makes it a perfect (and instructive) worked example. *(All figures approximate and rounded for teaching.)*

**The inputs:**
- Market capitalisation (E): ~₹2,70,000 crore
- Total debt (D): ~₹2,500 crore *(minor borrowings + lease liabilities)*
- Risk-free rate (Rf): 7% *(10-year G-Sec)*
- Beta (β): 0.8 *(defensive consumer staple — low volatility)*
- Equity risk premium (ERP): 6.5%
- Pre-tax cost of debt (Kd): 8% *(AAA-rated, borrows cheaply)*
- Tax rate: 25%

**Step 1 — Cost of equity (CAPM):**
Ke = Rf + β × ERP = 7% + 0.8 × 6.5% = 7% + 5.2% = **12.2%**

**Step 2 — After-tax cost of debt:**
Kd × (1 − t) = 8% × (1 − 0.25) = 8% × 0.75 = **6.0%**

**Step 3 — The market-value weights:**
V = E + D = 2,70,000 + 2,500 = ₹2,72,500 crore
E/V = 2,70,000 / 2,72,500 = **99.1%**
D/V = 2,500 / 2,72,500 = **0.9%**

**Step 4 — Blend into WACC:**
WACC = (E/V × Ke) + (D/V × Kd × (1−t))
WACC = (0.991 × 12.2%) + (0.009 × 6.0%)
WACC = 12.09% + 0.05% = **≈ 12.1%**

**The big lesson hiding in this number.** Notice that Asian Paints' WACC (12.1%) is **almost identical to its cost of equity (12.2%)**. Why? Because the company is ~99% equity-funded — the cheap debt is too small a slice to matter. This is a general truth worth internalising: **for a debt-free or near-debt-free company, WACC ≈ cost of equity.** It also means the tax shield (which only applies to debt) barely helps Asian Paints — a highly levered company would enjoy a much bigger WACC reduction from that shield.

And here's the contrast that makes the previous card real: if you had naïvely used Asian Paints' *book* equity (~₹14,000 cr) instead of its market cap, the debt weight would have jumped from 0.9% to ~15%, pulling the WACC down toward the cheaper 6% debt cost and *materially overvaluing* the company. The worked example and the mistake card are two sides of the same coin.

**🧠 Remember:** Asian Paints' WACC ≈ 12.1%, almost equal to its 12.2% cost of equity — because it's nearly debt-free, the rule "for a low-debt company, WACC ≈ Ke" holds, and the debt tax shield barely moves the needle.

---

### CARD 7

> 📊 **VISUAL: The one test that matters — ROCE above WACC, or below?**

*[Plain English description — what the student should imagine]*

WACC isn't just a discount rate — it's a **hurdle**. A company *creates* value only when it earns a return on capital (ROCE) *above* its cost of capital (WACC), and *destroys* value when it earns *below* it. Picture a vertical scale — a "value thermometer" — with a thick horizontal line drawn at **WACC = 12%**. Plot companies on it by their ROCE. High above the line sit the value compounders: a Nestlé India or Asian Paints earning 25–40% ROCE against a ~12% WACC — every rupee they invest *creates* value, and growth makes shareholders richer. Right at the line, value is merely preserved. *Below* the line sit the value destroyers: a struggling business or a capital-heavy laggard earning, say, 8% ROCE against a 12% WACC — every rupee it reinvests *destroys* value, and growth makes shareholders *poorer*. The gap between a company's ROCE and the WACC line — the **spread** — is the engine of value creation. This is the single most important check an analyst runs, and most students never run it.

**AI Image Generation Prompt:**
> Create a widescreen 16:9 financial infographic, dark cosmic aesthetic — deep navy background (#02073D) with a faint starfield. Title at top in clean white sans-serif: "THE ONLY TEST THAT MATTERS: ROCE vs WACC". Centre: a bold horizontal dividing line spanning the width, labelled in #FF6AC1 pink "WACC (Cost of Capital) = 12% — THE HURDLE". The zone ABOVE the line is tinted faint #246BFD blue/green and labelled "VALUE CREATED"; the zone BELOW is tinted faint red and labelled "VALUE DESTROYED". Plot four labelled dots: high above the line "Nestlé India — ROCE ~40%" and "Asian Paints — ROCE ~30%" in #6A5CFF purple; just above the line "Average company — ROCE ~14%"; and clearly below the line in red "Capital-heavy laggard — ROCE ~8%". For each dot above the line, a small upward green arrow labelled "growth → richer"; for the dot below, a downward red arrow labelled "growth → poorer". Right side: a vertical bracket showing "SPREAD = ROCE − WACC = value-creation engine". Captions in small JetBrains-Mono-style monospace. Bottom caption in #F5ECFF lavender: "Growth is only good when ROCE > WACC. Always run this test." Style: clean, modern, high-contrast, generous spacing, Bricolage-Grotesque-style headings.

**Why this visual works:** It turns WACC from an abstract discount rate into a *decision line* you can see — companies above it are worth owning through growth, companies below it are quietly destroying capital no matter how fast they grow. Placing real Indian names on both sides of the line makes the test concrete and memorable, and it directly extends the capital-allocation lesson from Session 12.3 (reinvesting below your cost of capital destroys value).

**🧠 Remember:** WACC is a hurdle, not just a discount rate — a company creates value only when ROCE > WACC and destroys it when ROCE < WACC, so the spread between return on capital and cost of capital is the single most important test an analyst runs.

---

### CARD 8

> 📝 **SESSION SUMMARY: What You Now Know**

```
🎯 The Big Idea:
WACC is the blended cost of feeding a company — cheap debt plus expensive equity,
weighted by how much of each it uses. It's the discount rate in your DCF AND the
hurdle that decides whether a business creates or destroys value.

✅ 5 Things You Now Know:
1. Equity is the MOST expensive capital — shareholders are paid last and bear the
   most risk, so Ke > Kd, always. "Free" equity is the costliest illusion in finance.
2. WACC = (E/V)·Ke + (D/V)·Kd·(1−t). Debt is cheaper than it looks because interest
   is tax-deductible (the tax shield); equity gets no such shield.
3. Cost of equity = Rf + β·ERP. In India: Rf ≈ 7% (G-Sec), ERP ≈ 6.5–7%, and use a
   stable BOTTOM-UP beta, not the noisy raw NSE regression beta.
4. Always use MARKET-value weights, never book — book equity can be a fraction of
   market cap, and book weights understate WACC and overvalue the company.
5. The one test that matters: ROCE > WACC = value created; ROCE < WACC = value
   destroyed. A company earning 8% with a 12% WACC is going broke in slow motion.

⚡ The Analyst Move:
A textbook student plugs an 11% WACC into every model. A working analyst builds the
WACC bottom-up, justifies every Indian input, uses market-value weights, runs a
sensitivity on it (because ±1% swings the valuation >10%), and ALWAYS checks ROCE
against WACC before trusting any growth story.

🔗 Connects to:
• Session 12.2 (DCF) — WACC is the "r" that discounts every cash flow.
• Session 12.5 (Terminal Value) — WACC sits in the (WACC − g) denominator that
  controls the majority of a DCF's value, so WACC errors are amplified there.
• Session 12.3 (FCF) — reinforces the capital-allocation test: reinvesting below
  WACC destroys value.

💬 If someone asks you "how do you calculate WACC?" at an interview, say:
"WACC is the weighted average of a company's cost of equity and after-tax cost of
debt, weighted by their market values. I'd get the cost of equity from CAPM — the
risk-free rate, around 7% on the 10-year G-Sec, plus a bottom-up beta times an
Indian equity risk premium of about 6.5 to 7%. For the cost of debt, I'd take the
borrowing rate and tax-affect it, since interest is deductible. Then I'd weight them
by market values — market cap for equity, not book — because WACC reflects what
investors demand today. And I'd always sanity-check it: if the company's ROCE is
below its WACC, it's destroying value no matter how fast it grows."
```

---
---

## QUIZ (5 QUESTIONS)

---

**Q1. What does the Weighted Average Cost of Capital (WACC) represent?**

A) The interest rate a company pays on its bank loans
B) The blended cost of all the capital (debt and equity) a company uses to fund itself — the minimum return its investments must earn
C) The company's net profit margin
D) The dividend a company pays to shareholders

**✅ Answer: B**

**Explanation:** WACC blends the cost of debt and the cost of equity, weighted by how much of each the company uses, to give the overall cost of "feeding" the business — which is both the discount rate in a DCF and the hurdle rate every investment must clear. Option A captures only the debt cost, ignoring the (larger and more expensive) equity cost. C and D confuse cost of capital with profitability and distributions, which are unrelated concepts.

**Difficulty:** Easy

---

**Q2. A stock has a beta of 1.2. The risk-free rate is 7% and the equity risk premium is 6%. Using CAPM, what is its cost of equity?**

A) 13.0%
B) 14.2%
C) 7.2%
D) 12.0%

**✅ Answer: B**

**Explanation:** Ke = Rf + β × ERP = 7% + 1.2 × 6% = 7% + 7.2% = 14.2%. Option A (13%) simply adds Rf and ERP, forgetting to scale the premium by beta. Option C (7.2%) is just β × ERP, omitting the risk-free base. The beta > 1 correctly pushes the cost of equity *above* what an average (β = 1) stock would demand, reflecting higher-than-market risk.

**Difficulty:** Medium

---

**Q3. Asian Paints (NSE: ASIANPAINT) is a nearly debt-free company. What does this imply about its WACC?**

A) Its WACC is roughly equal to its cost of equity, because the small debt slice barely affects the blend
B) Its WACC is roughly equal to its cost of debt
C) Its WACC is zero, because it has almost no debt
D) WACC cannot be calculated for a company with low debt

**✅ Answer: A**

**Explanation:** When a company is ~99% equity-funded, the equity weight dominates the WACC formula and the cheap-but-tiny debt slice is nearly irrelevant — so WACC ≈ cost of equity (for Asian Paints, ~12.1% vs a ~12.2% Ke). Option B is backwards (debt's weight is negligible, not dominant). C confuses low debt with low cost of capital — equity still has a substantial cost. D is false — WACC is perfectly calculable; it simply collapses toward Ke.

**Difficulty:** Medium

---

**Q4. An analyst computes WACC using book values for the equity and debt weights instead of market values. For a high-quality company, what is the most likely effect on the calculated WACC and resulting valuation?**

A) WACC is overstated and the company is undervalued
B) WACC is understated and the company is overvalued, because book equity is far below market cap, which overstates the (cheaper) debt's weight
C) There is no effect; book and market weights give the same answer
D) WACC becomes negative

**✅ Answer: B**

**Explanation:** For a quality company, book equity is a small fraction of market cap (Asian Paints: ~₹14,000 cr book vs ~₹2,70,000 cr market), so using book weights makes debt look like a far larger share of capital than it really is. Since debt is the cheaper capital, overweighting it pulls the WACC artificially *down*, which inflates the DCF value — overvaluing the company. Option A reverses the direction; C is false (book and market equity differ enormously); D is nonsensical here. Always use market-value weights.

**Difficulty:** Hard

---

**Q5. A company earns a return on capital employed (ROCE) of 8%, while its WACC is 12%. What does this tell you about the company's growth?**

A) Growth is good, because any positive ROCE creates value
B) Growth destroys value, because the company earns less on its capital (8%) than that capital costs (12%)
C) Growth is neutral and has no effect on value
D) The company should grow faster to improve the situation

**✅ Answer: B**

**Explanation:** Value is created only when ROCE exceeds WACC. Earning 8% on capital that costs 12% means every rupee deployed returns less than it costs, so each unit of growth makes shareholders *poorer* — value destruction dressed up as growth. Option A ignores the cost-of-capital hurdle entirely (the classic mistake). Option D is exactly wrong — growing *faster* while ROCE < WACC destroys value *faster*; the company should either fix its returns above WACC or return cash to shareholders rather than reinvest it.

**Difficulty:** Hard

---
---

## INTERVIEW QUESTIONS (3 QUESTIONS)

---

**IQ1. "Walk me through how you'd calculate WACC."**

*Difficulty:* Junior Analyst
*Type:* Technical
*Firm type likely to ask:* All

**💬 Strong answer:**
"WACC is the weighted average of a company's cost of equity and after-tax cost of debt, weighted by the market values of each. I'd start by splitting the capital structure — market cap for equity, and the value of debt — to get the equity and debt weights. For the cost of equity, I'd use CAPM: the risk-free rate, plus beta times the equity risk premium. For the cost of debt, I'd take the company's borrowing rate and multiply by one minus the tax rate, because interest is tax-deductible, which gives debt a tax shield. Then I'd plug both into the formula — equity weight times cost of equity, plus debt weight times after-tax cost of debt. One thing I'm careful about is using market values for the weights, not book values, because WACC reflects what investors demand today. And since a one-point change in WACC can swing a DCF by over ten percent, I'd run a sensitivity on it rather than treating it as a single fixed number."

**🚫 Weak answer trap:** Using book-value weights, or forgetting to tax-affect the cost of debt — both are immediate signals of a half-understood formula.

**✅ What this question tests:** Whether you can build WACC cleanly *and* flag the two things that trip people up (market vs book weights, the debt tax shield). It's a guaranteed question in equity research and IB interviews.

---

**IQ2. "How would you estimate the cost of equity for an Indian company, and what numbers would you actually use?"**

*Difficulty:* Senior Analyst
*Type:* Technical / Conceptual
*Firm type likely to ask:* Buy-side / Sell-side

**💬 Strong answer:**
"I'd use CAPM: cost of equity equals the risk-free rate plus beta times the equity risk premium. For the risk-free rate in India, I'd take the 10-year G-Sec yield, around 7% — though I'd acknowledge it's not perfectly risk-free, since India's sovereign rating embeds a small default premium. For the equity risk premium, India's is higher than developed markets because of country risk; I'd use something in the 6.5 to 7% range, ideally justified by Damodaran's approach of a mature-market premium plus an India country risk premium. For beta, I'd be wary of the raw regression beta off the NSE, because it's noisy and unstable for individual stocks — I'd prefer a bottom-up beta, taking comparable companies, unlevering their betas, averaging the business risk, and relevering for my company's own debt level. So for a defensive large-cap with a beta around 0.8, that's roughly 7% plus 0.8 times 6.5%, or about 12.2%. The key point is that each input is a defensible judgment, not a lookup, so I'd state my assumptions clearly."

**🚫 Weak answer trap:** Quoting CAPM but using a raw regression beta uncritically, or being unable to put actual Indian numbers (G-Sec ~7%, ERP ~6.5–7%) to the inputs — it signals classroom knowledge without market awareness.

**✅ What this question tests:** Whether you know CAPM *and* the India-specific estimation judgment around it — the risk-free-rate debate, the elevated ERP, and the case for bottom-up over regression beta. This is exactly the depth a sell-side or buy-side desk probes.

---

**IQ3. "Should you use book value or market value for the weights in WACC? Why does it matter?"**

*Difficulty:* Associate
*Type:* Technical / Judgment
*Firm type likely to ask:* All

**💬 Strong answer:**
"Market value, almost always. WACC is meant to capture what investors demand *today*, so the weights should reflect today's values of equity and debt — and for equity, that's the market cap, not the book value on the balance sheet. The reason it matters so much is that for any good company, book equity is a small fraction of market cap — Asian Paints, for instance, has book equity around ₹14,000 crore but a market cap near ₹2,70,000 crore. If I used book weights, debt would look like roughly 15% of capital instead of its true ~1%, and since debt is the cheaper source, that would drag the WACC down artificially and overvalue the company. For debt, book value is usually an acceptable proxy for market value since loans sit near par, but for the equity weight I'd never use book value. It's one of the most common WACC errors, and it always biases the valuation upward for quality companies."

**🚫 Weak answer trap:** "Book value, because that's what's on the balance sheet" — a clear tell that the candidate doesn't understand WACC reflects current required returns, not historical accounting.

**✅ What this question tests:** Whether you understand *why* WACC uses market values — that it's a forward-looking measure of investor-required returns — and whether you grasp the directional valuation impact of getting it wrong. A favourite discriminating question.

---

*End of Session 12.4 — WACC: The Cost of Feeding a Company's Hunger*
