<div style="page-break-after: always;"></div>

# MODULE 12 — SESSION 12.5: Terminal Value — The Number That Controls Everything

> *You spend three weeks forecasting five years of cash flows in obsessive, line-by-line detail. Then, in the final two minutes, you type one formula for the terminal value — and that single formula decides 70–80% of your answer. Most analysts find this terrifying. By the end of this session, you'll find it liberating.*

---

## SESSION OVERVIEW

**What you'll learn:** Why terminal value is the most important number in any DCF — and how to compute it honestly. We'll derive the **Gordon Growth Model** as nothing more than the present value of a growing perpetuity (the IOU we left in Session 12.2), nail down what terminal growth rate is *legitimate* (and why it can't exceed the economy's growth forever), learn the **exit-multiple** method as a cross-check, build the deeper **ROIC-based** terminal value that ties growth to value creation, and confront the cases where the whole "going concern forever" assumption simply breaks. The centrepiece is a full **WACC × g sensitivity table** that shows, with real numbers, just how violently terminal value swings.

**Why it matters:** Terminal value typically *is* the DCF — 60–80% of the total enterprise value. It also sits inside the (WACC − g) denominator, where the WACC errors from Session 12.4 get *amplified*. Interviewers probe terminal value harder than any other part of a DCF precisely because it's where over-optimism hides. Master it and the DCF stops being scary.

**Session difficulty:** ★★★★★ *(The single highest-leverage two minutes in all of valuation. Get this wrong and the other three weeks of work don't matter.)*

**Damodaran insight of the session:** The terminal value is where analysts smuggle in their hopes. Because it's a perpetuity, a tiny nudge to the growth rate creates enormous swings in value — which is exactly why it must be the *most* disciplined, not the least examined, part of your model. In Indian terms: assume a company grows at 8% forever and you're quietly claiming it will one day be larger than the entire Indian economy. The maths doesn't allow it, even if your optimism does.

---

## NOTES (8 CARDS)

---

### CARD 1

> 🎣 **HOOK — The two minutes that decide three weeks of work.**

Here's the uncomfortable truth about every DCF you'll ever build: **the terminal value usually represents 60–80% of the total valuation.** Read that again. Five years of painstaking forecasting — modelling revenue drivers, margins, working capital, capex, quarter by quarter — typically drives only about a quarter of your final answer. The *other three-quarters* comes from one formula you type at the very end to capture "everything after year five."

Most students react to this with horror. *"You mean the part I spent the least time on matters the most? That can't be right — the model must be broken."* But it isn't broken. It's a mathematical inevitability, and understanding *why* is the moment the DCF stops being intimidating.

Think about it. A healthy company doesn't stop existing in year five — that's just where your detailed forecast ends. It keeps generating cash for decades beyond. The terminal value bundles *all* of those future decades into a single number sitting at year five. Since "all the years after five" vastly outnumber "the five years before it," of course they're worth more. A company is a long-lived thing; your forecast window is short. The terminal value is simply the long tail, collapsed.

This has one massive implication: **the terminal value deserves your *most* careful thinking, not your least.** The two inputs that drive it — the long-term growth rate (g) and the discount rate (WACC) — must be defended more rigorously than any single-year revenue assumption. A careless 1% nudge to either can swing the entire valuation by double digits (you'll see exactly how much in Card 5).

So here's the reframe that liberates you: once you understand that the terminal value *is* the DCF, you stop fearing the model and start respecting the few assumptions that actually matter. The DCF isn't a 200-row monster. It's really a bet on two numbers — long-run growth and the cost of capital — wearing an elaborate spreadsheet as a disguise.

**🧠 Remember:** Terminal value is typically 60–80% of a DCF's total value, so the two-minute terminal calculation matters more than weeks of explicit forecasting — which means it deserves the most scrutiny, not the least.

---

### CARD 2

> 🔍 **DEEP DIVE: Deriving Gordon Growth — and the deeper ROIC version most analysts never learn.**

In Session 12.2 I asked you to *trust* the terminal value formula. Time to pay that IOU and derive it. The **Gordon Growth Model** is just the present value of a **growing perpetuity** — a stream of cash that grows at a constant rate g forever.

**The derivation (it's a geometric series).** Suppose a cash flow grows at g% each year and you discount at r%. Its present value is:
PV = CF₁/(1+r) + CF₁(1+g)/(1+r)² + CF₁(1+g)²/(1+r)³ + …
This is an infinite geometric series with first term CF₁/(1+r) and common ratio (1+g)/(1+r). The sum of an infinite geometric series is *first term ÷ (1 − ratio)*, which simplifies — beautifully — to:

> **PV of a growing perpetuity = CF₁ / (r − g)**

That's it. No magic. Now apply it at the *end of year n* to value all cash flows from year n+1 onwards: the first of those is FCFₙ₊₁ = FCFₙ × (1+g), so:

> **Terminal Value (at year n) = FCFₙ × (1 + g) / (WACC − g)**

**Two steps people forget.** First, you must *grow* the final forecast year's cash flow by (1+g) — the perpetuity starts the year *after* your forecast ends. Second — the classic blunder — this TV sits at year n and must itself be **discounted back to today**: PV of TV = TVₙ / (1+WACC)ⁿ. Forget that and you overstate value massively.

**Why (WACC − g) in the denominator is so dangerous.** As g approaches WACC, the denominator approaches zero and the terminal value explodes toward infinity. This is the mathematical reason terminal value is so sensitive — and why g must stay comfortably below WACC.

**The deeper version (for advanced students).** The simple Gordon model has a hidden flaw: it ignores that *growth requires reinvestment*. To grow at g, a company must plough back a fraction of its profit — and that fraction depends on how efficiently it reinvests (its ROIC): reinvestment rate = g / ROIC. So steady-state free cash flow is NOPAT × (1 − g/ROIC), giving the richer formula:

> **Terminal Value = NOPAT × (1 − g/ROIC) / (WACC − g)**

This reveals something profound that connects straight to Session 12.4. Plug in ROIC = WACC and the g terms *cancel* — TV becomes simply NOPAT/WACC, **completely independent of growth.** Meaning: when a company earns exactly its cost of capital, growth adds *zero* value. If ROIC > WACC, more growth raises TV (value-creating growth). If ROIC < WACC, more growth *lowers* TV (value-destroying growth). Growth is only worth assuming if the company earns above its cost of capital — the same truth from the ROCE-vs-WACC test, now baked into the terminal value itself.

**🧠 Remember:** Gordon Growth TV = FCFₙ(1+g)/(WACC−g) is just the present value of a growing perpetuity — and the deeper ROIC version proves growth only adds terminal value when ROIC exceeds WACC; otherwise growth is worthless or actively destructive.

---

### CARD 3

> 💡 **CONCEPT: The terminal growth rate — why a company can't outgrow the economy forever.**

The single most abused input in any DCF is the terminal growth rate, g. It looks innocent — just a number in one cell — but because it drives a perpetuity, getting it wrong wrecks the valuation. So what's *legitimate*?

**The iron rule: g must be ≤ the long-run nominal growth rate of the economy.** Here's the unarguable logic. If a company grew *faster* than the economy *forever*, then given enough time it would become *larger than the entire economy* — every other business, every farm, every shop combined. That's mathematically impossible. A single company can outgrow GDP for a decade or two, but *not in perpetuity*. So the terminal growth rate — which applies forever — is capped by the economy's long-run growth.

**What number for India?** This is where students stumble. India's *current* nominal GDP growth is high — roughly 10–11% (≈6–7% real + ~4–5% inflation). But the terminal growth rate isn't about *today's* economy; it's about the very-long-run, mature, steady-state economy decades out, which will grow more slowly as India develops. So you do **not** plug in 10–11%. In practice, analysts use a terminal g of around **4–6%** for Indian companies. A clean, defensible upper bound is **Damodaran's rule of thumb: terminal g should not exceed the risk-free rate** (~7% in India), because the risk-free rate is itself a decent proxy for long-run nominal economic growth in a mature economy.

**The thing that surprises most students:** the terminal growth rate has a *smaller* effect on a great company's value than they expect, and a *larger* effect on the model's stability than they expect. Pushing g from 5% to 6% feels minor, but as Card 5 will show, it can move the terminal value by 20%+ — because the denominator (WACC − g) shrinks. The discipline: pick a g you can defend as a *forever* number — usually inflation plus a touch of real growth — and never set it within striking distance of your WACC.

**A practical sanity check:** a mature consumer-staples company might justify a terminal g of ~5–6% (it can roughly keep pace with nominal GDP through pricing and modest volume growth). A declining or commoditised business deserves a *lower* g, perhaps 2–3% or even inflation-only. The terminal g encodes your view of the company's *perpetual* competitive position — choose it as a statement about the business, not a plug to hit a target price.

**🧠 Remember:** Terminal growth must be ≤ long-run nominal GDP growth (else the company eventually becomes the whole economy) — for India that means ~4–6% in practice, capped by the ~7% risk-free rate, never today's high nominal GDP figure and never near your WACC.

---

### CARD 4

> 💡 **CONCEPT: The exit-multiple method — and using it to cross-check Gordon Growth.**

There's a second way to compute terminal value, and serious analysts use it *alongside* Gordon Growth as a reality check. Instead of treating the company as a perpetuity, the **exit-multiple method** asks: *what would a buyer pay for this business at the end of my forecast, based on market multiples?*

> **Terminal Value = Terminal-Year EBITDA × Exit Multiple (e.g., terminal EV/EBITDA)**

If a company's year-5 EBITDA is ₹2,000 crore and comparable mature businesses trade at ~12× EV/EBITDA, the terminal value is 12 × 2,000 = ₹24,000 crore (sitting at year 5, then discounted back like always).

**When to use it.** The exit-multiple method dominates in private equity and LBO modelling, because PE firms genuinely *exit* by selling at a market multiple — so it mirrors reality. In equity research, it's most useful as a **cross-check** on the Gordon Growth output. The two methods approach the same question from opposite ends — one from intrinsic cash flows, one from market pricing — and if they roughly agree, you can trust your terminal value more. If they wildly disagree, your assumptions are internally inconsistent and you need to find out why.

**The honest caveat.** An exit multiple is itself a *shortcut DCF* (recall Session 12.1) — a multiple already embeds assumptions about growth and risk. So using one inside a DCF is a little circular, and it imports today's market mood into a number that's years away. Picking the multiple is also subjective. That's why it's a *cross-check*, not a sole method, in fundamental research.

**The pro move — back out the implied numbers.** The slickest sanity check is to translate between the two methods. From your Gordon Growth assumptions (g and WACC), compute the *implied* exit multiple, and ask: "Is it reasonable that this business trades at, say, 18× in perpetuity?" Or reverse it: from your chosen exit multiple, back out the *implied* perpetuity growth rate and check it isn't absurd (e.g., an implied g of 8% should set off alarms). When an analyst at a Motilal Oswal or Nuvama presents a terminal value, this two-way reconciliation is exactly what a sharp senior will ask for. If your Gordon g implies a 30× exit multiple, one of your assumptions is fantasy.

**🧠 Remember:** The exit-multiple method (terminal EBITDA × an exit multiple) is the market-based alternative to Gordon Growth — best used as a cross-check, where you reconcile the two by backing out the implied multiple or implied growth rate to expose unreasonable assumptions.

---

### CARD 5

> 📊 **VISUAL: The terminal-value sensitivity table — watch one assumption move everything.**

*[Plain English description — what the student should imagine]*

Here is the single most important exhibit in equity research: a grid with the **discount rate (WACC) across the top** and the **terminal growth rate (g) down the side**, with the resulting terminal value in every cell. Picture it as a *heat map* — cool blue in the bottom-right (high WACC, low g → small terminal value) blazing to hot pink in the top-left (low WACC, high g → enormous terminal value). The same business, valued across a plausible range of assumptions, produces a *3× spread* in terminal value. The lesson hits you in the eyes: terminal value isn't a number, it's a *range*, and tiny assumption changes move it violently.

The table below uses a terminal-year free cash flow of **₹1,000 crore** and computes the Gordon terminal value, **TV = 1,000 × (1+g) / (WACC − g)**, for each combination (₹ crore, sitting at the terminal year):

| g ↓ / WACC → | 10% | 11% | 12% | 13% | 14% |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **2%** | 12,750 | 11,333 | 10,200 | 9,273 | 8,500 |
| **3%** | 14,714 | 12,875 | 11,444 | 10,300 | 9,364 |
| **4%** | 17,333 | 14,857 | **13,000** | 11,556 | 10,400 |
| **5%** | 21,000 | **17,500** | 15,000 | 13,125 | 11,667 |
| **6%** | 26,500 | 21,200 | 17,667 | 15,143 | 13,250 |

Read what this means. The **base case** (g = 4%, WACC = 12%) gives ₹13,000 cr. Now make a *modest, entirely plausible* shift — nudge g up by one point to 5% and WACC down by one point to 11% — and the terminal value jumps to ₹17,500 cr, a **+35%** swing from changing each input by a single percentage point. And the full corner-to-corner range, from the pessimistic ₹8,500 cr (g 2%, WACC 14%) to the optimistic ₹26,500 cr (g 6%, WACC 10%), is more than **3×**. *This* is why a point estimate is a lie and a sensitivity table is mandatory in every credible research report.

**AI Image Generation Prompt:**
> Create a widescreen 16:9 financial infographic, dark cosmic aesthetic — deep navy background (#02073D) with a faint starfield. Title at top in clean white sans-serif: "TERMINAL VALUE SENSITIVITY — WHY A POINT ESTIMATE IS A LIE". Render a 5×5 heat-map grid: columns labelled "WACC 10%, 11%, 12%, 13%, 14%" along the top, rows labelled "g = 2%, 3%, 4%, 5%, 6%" down the left, all labels in #F5ECFF lavender monospace. Fill each cell with the terminal-value figure (₹ cr): top-left corner cells (low WACC, high g) glow hot #FF6AC1 pink with the largest numbers (26,500 at g6/WACC10); bottom-right cells (high WACC, low g) are cool deep #246BFD blue with the smallest numbers (8,500 at g2/WACC14); a smooth gradient runs diagonally between. Highlight the centre base-case cell (g4/WACC12 = 13,000) with a thin white border labelled "BASE", and the g5/WACC11 cell (17,500) with a #6A5CFF purple glow and a small callout "+35% from a 1-point shift in EACH". Bottom caption in lavender: "Same business. 3× range. Always show the table." Style: clean, modern, high-contrast, generous cell spacing, Bricolage-Grotesque-style headings.

**Why this visual works:** It converts an abstract warning ("terminal value is sensitive") into a grid you can *feel* — the colour gradient and the 3× spread between corners make the danger physical, and highlighting the +35% jump from a one-point shift proves that honest valuation must be a range. Every analyst who has built one of these stops trusting single-point target prices forever.

**🧠 Remember:** A small, plausible change in g and WACC (each by one point) swung terminal value by ~35%, and the full range spans more than 3× — which is exactly why a two-way WACC × g sensitivity table is mandatory and a single-point terminal value is dishonest.

---

### CARD 6

> ⚠️ **THE MISTAKE: Applying a "going concern" perpetuity to a business that won't go on forever.**

Every Gordon Growth terminal value rests on a silent assumption: that the business is a **going concern** — it operates *forever*, generating ever-growing cash into perpetuity. For most healthy companies (an HUL, a TCS), that's reasonable. But applying a perpetuity to a business with a *finite life* is a serious — and surprisingly common — error.

**Why it's so easy to make:** the Gordon formula is the default, taught as *the* way to compute terminal value, so students apply it reflexively to every company without asking the prior question: *will this business actually exist forever?* The formula doesn't warn you; it cheerfully produces a large, confident number even when a perpetuity makes no sense.

**Where it breaks — three cases.**
1. **Finite-life assets.** A coal mine, an iron-ore deposit, an oil field, a single-asset toll road with an expiry — these have a *fixed quantity* of reserves or a *fixed concession period*. A coal mine with 20 years of reserves does not generate cash for eternity; it generates cash for 20 years and then it's a hole in the ground. Slapping a growing perpetuity on it overstates value enormously. The right tool is to value the *finite* stream of cash flows over the asset's actual life (and any salvage/closure costs), not a perpetuity. (This is the **reserve-based valuation** logic we'll develop in Session 12.14.)
2. **Distressed companies.** A firm bleeding cash and at risk of bankruptcy may not survive five years, let alone forever. A going-concern perpetuity assumes a future the company may never reach; the relevant floor is often *liquidation value*, not a perpetuity (again, Session 12.14).
3. **Structurally declining businesses.** A company in terminal decline (think a fading legacy technology) shouldn't get a *positive* growth perpetuity at all — at best a low or *negative* terminal growth rate, or a finite wind-down.

**The correct approach:** before computing a Gordon terminal value, ask *"is a perpetuity even appropriate here?"* If the business has finite reserves, a fixed concession, or genuine survival risk, switch to a finite-horizon model or an asset-based floor. For a company like **Coal India**, valuing its mines as eternal growing perpetuities would be conceptually wrong — its coal reserves are large but *finite*, so reserve-based valuation is more honest than a textbook Gordon TV.

**What the interviewer thinks:** a candidate who mechanically applies Gordon Growth to a mining company signals *"learned one formula, doesn't think about whether it fits."* A candidate who says "I'd check whether a perpetuity is even valid before applying Gordon Growth — for a finite-reserve miner I'd value the reserves over their actual life" signals genuine judgment.

**🧠 Remember:** Gordon Growth assumes the business lives forever — so for finite-life assets (mines, oil fields, expiring concessions), distressed firms, or declining businesses, a growing perpetuity badly overstates value, and you must switch to a finite-horizon or asset-based approach.

---

### CARD 7

> 🌍 **REAL WORLD: Watching terminal value dominate a stable FMCG compounder.**

**Archetype:** A stable consumer-staples compounder — think Hindustan Unilever (NSE: HINDUNILVR) or Nestlé India (NSE: NESTLEIND) · Sector: FMCG

Let's make the "terminal value is 70–80% of the DCF" claim concrete on the kind of predictable, long-lived business where a DCF actually works well. *(Numbers below are simplified and illustrative — designed to show the structure, not to match the company's exact market value.)*

Picture a stable FMCG company whose **free cash flow grows from ~₹9,000 crore in year 1 to ~₹12,000 crore by year 5**, discounted at a **WACC of 11%**, with a defensible terminal growth rate of **5%** (an FMCG can roughly keep pace with nominal GDP through pricing power and steady volume growth — a legitimate forever number).

**The explicit period:** discounting each year's growing FCF back at 11%, the present value of the five explicit years sums to **≈ ₹38,000 crore**.

**The terminal value:**
- TV at year 5 = FCF₅ × (1+g) / (WACC − g) = 12,000 × 1.05 / (0.11 − 0.05) = 12,600 / 0.06 = **₹2,10,000 crore**
- Discount back to today: PV of TV = 2,10,000 / (1.11)⁵ = 2,10,000 / 1.685 = **≈ ₹1,24,600 crore**

**The split:**
- Enterprise Value = 38,000 + 1,24,600 = **≈ ₹1,62,600 crore**
- Terminal value as % of EV = 1,24,600 / 1,62,600 = **≈ 77%**

There it is — **77% of this company's value sits in the terminal value**, exactly as the hook promised. The five years you'd forecast in obsessive detail drive only ~23% of the answer; the perpetuity drives the rest. This is *normal and correct* for a high-quality, long-lived business, and it's precisely why your terminal growth and WACC assumptions deserve more scrutiny than any single quarter's revenue.

**One honest footnote that links back to Session 12.2:** in reality, premium compounders like HUL and Nestlé India often trade *above* what a conservative DCF like this produces — because the market is pricing an even longer, higher-quality growth runway than a cautious five-year-plus-perpetuity model captures. That gap isn't necessarily the market being wrong; it can be the market seeing durability your timid DCF refuses to. The terminal value is where that disagreement lives.

**The lesson for all stable compounders:** for predictable, long-lived businesses, the terminal value will *always* dominate — so the real valuation debate isn't about next year's numbers, it's about how long and how profitably the company can grow in perpetuity. Win that argument and you've won the valuation.

**🧠 Remember:** For a stable FMCG compounder, ~77% of the DCF value lives in the terminal value — proving that for high-quality long-lived businesses the valuation hinges almost entirely on the perpetuity assumptions, not the explicit forecast years.

---

### CARD 8

> 📝 **SESSION SUMMARY: What You Now Know**

```
🎯 The Big Idea:
Terminal value is 60–80% of a DCF — the two-minute calculation that decides most
of your answer. It's the present value of a growing perpetuity, and it's where
analysts hide their optimism, so it demands the MOST discipline, not the least.

✅ 5 Things You Now Know:
1. Gordon Growth TV = FCFₙ(1+g)/(WACC−g) is just the PV of a growing perpetuity.
   Grow the final year's FCF by (1+g), AND remember to discount the TV back to today.
2. Terminal growth g must be ≤ long-run nominal GDP — else the company eventually
   becomes the whole economy. For India: ~4–6%, capped by the ~7% risk-free rate.
3. The exit-multiple method (terminal EBITDA × exit multiple) is the market-based
   cross-check — reconcile it with Gordon by backing out the implied multiple or growth.
4. Terminal value is brutally sensitive: a 1-point shift in g AND WACC moved it ~35%,
   and the plausible range spanned 3× — so a two-way sensitivity table is mandatory.
5. Gordon assumes a going concern forever; for finite-life assets (mines), distressed
   firms, or declining businesses, a perpetuity overstates value — use finite or
   asset-based approaches instead.

⚡ The Analyst Move:
A textbook student types one Gordon formula and moves on. A working analyst (a) checks
a perpetuity is even valid, (b) cross-checks Gordon against an exit multiple, (c) backs
out the implied growth/multiple for a sanity check, (d) confirms ROIC > WACC so growth
actually adds value, and (e) ALWAYS presents the WACC × g sensitivity table.

🔗 Connects to:
• Session 12.2 (DCF) — this pays off the IOU; Gordon TV is the "TV" term you trusted.
• Session 12.4 (WACC) — WACC sits in the (WACC−g) denominator, so its errors are AMPLIFIED here.
• Session 12.6 (Full DCF) — next we build the whole model end-to-end on Pidilite.
• Session 12.14 (Asset-Based) — where going-concern fails, reserve/liquidation value takes over.

💬 If someone asks you "how do you handle terminal value?" at an interview, say:
"Terminal value is usually 60–80% of a DCF, so I treat it as the most important
assumption, not an afterthought. I use the Gordon Growth model — the final year's free
cash flow grown by a long-term rate, divided by WACC minus that growth rate — and I keep
the growth rate at or below long-run nominal GDP, around 4–6% in India and never above the
risk-free rate. I cross-check it against an exit multiple and back out the implied growth to
make sure it's sane. And because it's so sensitive — a one-point change in growth or WACC can
move it 30%-plus — I always present a WACC-versus-growth sensitivity table rather than a single
number. I'd also check the business is actually a going concern before assuming a perpetuity."
```

## QUIZ (5 QUESTIONS)

---

**Q1. In a DCF on a stable FMCG company like HUL, the terminal value works out to roughly 77% of enterprise value. What does this primarily imply?**

A) The model is broken — terminal value should never exceed 50%
B) The valuation hinges mostly on the long-term (perpetuity) assumptions, so the terminal growth rate and WACC deserve the most scrutiny
C) The first five years of forecasting are completely irrelevant
D) The company is overvalued

**✅ Answer: B**

**Explanation:** For a high-quality, long-lived business, terminal value dominating at 60–80% is normal — it captures all the decades beyond the explicit forecast — so the real valuation debate is about long-run growth and the discount rate, which deserve the deepest scrutiny. Option A invents a false rule (a 77% share is entirely typical). Option C overstates it — the explicit years still feed the terminal value and matter. D draws an unrelated conclusion; a high TV share says nothing about over- or undervaluation.

**Difficulty:** Easy

---

**Q2. A company's free cash flow in the final forecast year (Year 5) is ₹500 crore. Using a terminal growth rate of 4% and a WACC of 11%, what is the terminal value at the end of Year 5?**

A) ₹7,143 crore
B) ₹7,429 crore
C) ₹4,545 crore
D) ₹13,000 crore

**✅ Answer: B**

**Explanation:** TV = FCF₅ × (1+g) / (WACC − g) = 500 × 1.04 / (0.11 − 0.04) = 520 / 0.07 = ₹7,429 crore. Option A (₹7,143) forgets to grow the final year's FCF by (1+g), using 500/0.07 instead of 520/0.07 — the most common error. Option C (₹4,545) wrongly uses WACC alone (500/0.11) in the denominator instead of (WACC − g). D is unrelated. Note this TV sits at Year 5 and must still be discounted back to today.

**Difficulty:** Medium

---

**Q3. Why must the terminal growth rate (g) used in a DCF be no greater than the long-run nominal growth rate of the economy?**

A) Because tax rules require it
B) Because a company growing faster than the economy forever would eventually become larger than the entire economy, which is impossible
C) Because regulators cap growth rates at GDP
D) Because higher growth rates are harder to type into Excel

**✅ Answer: B**

**Explanation:** The terminal growth rate applies *in perpetuity*, and any company compounding faster than GDP forever would, given infinite time, exceed the size of the whole economy — a mathematical impossibility. A company can outgrow GDP for a decade or two, but not eternally, so g is capped by long-run nominal GDP (and in practice by the risk-free rate as a proxy). Options A, C, and D are fabricated or frivolous reasons with no basis.

**Difficulty:** Medium

---

**Q4. An analyst values Coal India (NSE: COALINDIA) by applying a standard Gordon Growth perpetuity to its mining cash flows, assuming they grow forever. Why is this conceptually flawed?**

A) Coal India is too large for a DCF
B) A coal mine has finite reserves — it generates cash only until the coal runs out — so a "grows forever" perpetuity overstates the value of a finite-life asset
C) Mining companies cannot be valued at all
D) The terminal growth rate for miners must always be negative

**✅ Answer: B**

**Explanation:** The Gordon model assumes a going concern operating into perpetuity, but a mine holds a *fixed quantity* of reserves and stops producing once they're depleted — so its cash flows are finite, not eternal. Valuing a finite-reserve asset as a growing perpetuity overstates its worth; the honest approach is reserve-based valuation over the asset's actual life. Option A is false (size doesn't preclude a DCF), C is too absolute (miners are valued via reserve-based methods), and D overstates a rule that isn't universal.

**Difficulty:** Hard

---

**Q5. Using the deeper ROIC-based terminal value formula, what happens to terminal value as the growth rate (g) changes if a company's ROIC exactly equals its WACC?**

A) Terminal value rises sharply with higher g
B) Terminal value is unaffected by g — growth adds no value when ROIC equals WACC
C) Terminal value falls to zero
D) Terminal value becomes negative

**✅ Answer: B**

**Explanation:** In the formula TV = NOPAT × (1 − g/ROIC) / (WACC − g), setting ROIC = WACC causes the g terms to cancel, leaving TV = NOPAT / WACC — completely independent of growth. This proves a deep truth: when a company earns exactly its cost of capital, growth creates *no* value (every reinvested rupee returns precisely what it costs). Option A would only be true if ROIC > WACC (value-creating growth); growth reduces value only when ROIC < WACC. C and D don't follow from the maths.

**Difficulty:** Hard

---
---

## INTERVIEW QUESTIONS (3 QUESTIONS)

---

**IQ1. "How do you calculate terminal value, and what methods do you know?"**

*Difficulty:* Junior Analyst
*Type:* Technical
*Firm type likely to ask:* All

**💬 Strong answer:**
"There are two main methods. The first is the Gordon Growth, or perpetuity growth, model — I take the final forecast year's free cash flow, grow it by a long-term growth rate, and divide by WACC minus that growth rate. It's really just the present value of a growing perpetuity. The second is the exit-multiple method — I apply a terminal EV/EBITDA multiple to the final year's EBITDA, which is what private equity tends to use because it mirrors an actual sale. In equity research I'd usually compute the Gordon Growth value and then cross-check it against an exit multiple to make sure they're broadly consistent. Two things I'm careful about: I grow the final year's cash flow by one plus g before applying the perpetuity, and I always discount the terminal value back to today, since it sits at the end of the forecast, not in the present. People forget that second step and massively overstate value."

**🚫 Weak answer trap:** Forgetting to discount the terminal value back to the present, or forgetting to grow the final-year cash flow by (1+g) — both are classic mechanical errors that inflate the valuation.

**✅ What this question tests:** Whether you know both methods, can execute the Gordon mechanics correctly, and instinctively cross-check. It's asked in virtually every DCF-focused interview.

---

**IQ2. "What terminal growth rate would you use for an Indian company, and why can't it exceed nominal GDP growth?"**

*Difficulty:* Senior Analyst
*Type:* Conceptual
*Firm type likely to ask:* Buy-side / Sell-side

**💬 Strong answer:**
"I'd typically use something in the 4 to 6% range for an Indian company, and I'd cap it at the risk-free rate, around 7%, as a clean upper bound. The reason it can't exceed long-run nominal GDP growth is simple but absolute: the terminal rate applies forever, and if a company grew faster than the economy in perpetuity, it would eventually become larger than the entire economy, which is impossible. One nuance I'd flag is that India's *current* nominal GDP growth is around 10 to 11%, but that's not the right number for a terminal rate — the terminal rate is about the mature, steady-state economy decades out, which grows more slowly as the country develops. So I'd anchor to long-run inflation plus modest real growth, choose a g I can defend as a genuine forever number, and keep it well clear of my WACC, because as g approaches WACC the perpetuity explodes."

**🚫 Weak answer trap:** Plugging in India's current ~10–11% nominal GDP growth as the terminal rate, or setting g uncomfortably close to WACC — both reveal a misunderstanding of what a perpetuity rate represents.

**✅ What this question tests:** Whether you understand the economic logic constraining terminal growth and can translate it into a defensible Indian number — not just recite "4 to 6%."

---

**IQ3. "When would the Gordon Growth perpetuity be the wrong way to compute terminal value?"**

*Difficulty:* Associate
*Type:* Judgment
*Firm type likely to ask:* All

**💬 Strong answer:**
"Whenever the business isn't genuinely a going concern that operates forever, which the Gordon model silently assumes. The clearest case is finite-life assets — a coal mine, an oil field, or a toll road with an expiring concession. A mine with 20 years of reserves doesn't generate cash into eternity; it produces until the reserves run out and then stops, so a growing perpetuity badly overstates its value. There I'd value the finite stream of cash flows over the asset's actual life, or use reserve-based valuation. The second case is a distressed company that may not even survive the forecast period — a perpetuity assumes a future it might never reach, so liquidation value becomes the more relevant floor. And the third is a structurally declining business, where assuming positive perpetual growth makes no sense; at best it gets a very low or negative terminal rate, or a finite wind-down. So before I apply Gordon Growth, I always ask whether a perpetuity is even appropriate for *this* business."

**🚫 Weak answer trap:** Treating Gordon Growth as universally applicable, or not being able to name a concrete case (like finite-reserve mining) where it fails — it signals formula-memorisation over judgment.

**✅ What this question tests:** Whether you think critically about *whether a method fits the company* rather than applying it reflexively — exactly the judgment that separates an associate from a junior, and a direct bridge to asset-based valuation (Session 12.14).

---

*End of Session 12.5 — Terminal Value: The Number That Controls Everything*

# MODULE 12 — SESSION 12.6: Building the Full DCF — Putting It All Together

> *Pidilite makes Fevicol — the adhesive whose tagline promised a bond so strong it could never break. Pidilite's competitive moat is just as unbreakable. But a great business and a great* investment *are two different things — and the only way to tell them apart is to build the full model. So let's assemble every piece you've learned, value Pidilite end to end, and find out what the market is really asking you to believe.*

---

## SESSION OVERVIEW

**What you'll learn:** How to assemble a complete DCF from scratch — the integrative session where FCF (12.3), WACC (12.4), and terminal value (12.5) finally come together into one working model. We'll build it on a real company, **Pidilite Industries**, step by step: forecast revenue with a driver-based approach, project free cash flow line by line, compute the WACC, discount every cash flow, calculate the terminal value, bridge from Enterprise Value down to intrinsic value *per share*, compare to the market price, and stress-test the answer. The summary card hands you a memorisable DCF cheat sheet.

**Why it matters:** This is the skill the entire module has been building toward — and the single most common technical task in equity research and IB. When an interviewer says "value this company," they want to watch you do *exactly this*, cleanly and in order. Build it once with full understanding and you own it forever.

**Session difficulty:** ★★★★★ *(Everything converges here. If any earlier session was shaky, this is where it shows — and where it gets fixed.)*

**Damodaran insight of the session:** A finished DCF isn't an answer — it's a question. It tells you precisely what you'd have to believe about growth, margins, and risk for today's price to make sense. The most valuable output isn't the per-share number; it's the *list of assumptions the market is implicitly making*. In Indian terms: when Pidilite's DCF lands far below its price, the model hasn't failed — it's telling you the market is betting on a far longer compounding runway than your cautious forecast, and now you get to decide whether that bet is sane.

---

## NOTES (8 CARDS)

---

### CARD 1

> 🎣 **HOOK — A great business is not the same as a great investment.**

Ask any Indian investor to name a quality company and Pidilite Industries will be near the top of the list. It owns Fevicol, Dr. Fixit, M-Seal, Fevikwik — brands so dominant that "Fevicol" is practically the Hindi word for glue. Its moat is textbook: an unmatched distribution network reaching millions of carpenters and contractors, deep brand loyalty, pricing power, and a near-debt-free balance sheet. By every qualitative measure, it's a wonderful business.

Here's the trap that catches even smart people: **they confuse a wonderful business with a wonderful investment.** Those are *different questions*. "Is this a great company?" is about the business. "Should I buy the stock at today's price?" is about *valuation* — and a great company bought at the wrong price can be a terrible investment. Pidilite has traded at a Price-to-Earnings multiple of 70–90× for years. At that price, you're not just buying a great business — you're paying *in advance* for a great deal of future growth. The question is whether that growth will actually show up.

The only honest way to answer is to build the full DCF. Not a vibe ("it's quality, so buy"), not a multiple plucked from the air — a complete model that forces you to state, explicitly, how fast you think Pidilite grows, what it earns, and how risky those rupees are. Then you compare your number to the market's number, and the *gap* tells you what the market believes.

This session is the payoff for everything. You learned to build free cash flow in 12.3, the discount rate in 12.4, and the terminal value in 12.5. Now you'll wire all three together on a real company, line by line, and produce an intrinsic value per share you can defend out loud. And the result — as you'll see — will say something genuinely surprising about one of India's most beloved stocks.

**🧠 Remember:** A wonderful business and a wonderful investment are different questions — only a full DCF, built from explicit assumptions, can tell you whether a great company like Pidilite is actually worth its price.

---

### CARD 2

> 🌍 **REAL WORLD: Pidilite — Step 1, forecasting revenue the right way.**

**Company:** Pidilite Industries Ltd · Sector: Adhesives / Specialty Chemicals · **NSE: PIDILITIND**

Every DCF starts with revenue, and *how* you forecast it separates analysts from guessers. The lazy way is to slap a single growth rate on the top line. The right way is **driver-based**: revenue growth = **volume growth + price growth**. You forecast the two engines separately because they behave differently and tell different stories.

For Pidilite, the drivers are:
- **Volume growth (~7–8%):** more glue, sealants, and waterproofing sold, driven by India's construction and home-improvement boom, rising rural penetration, and Pidilite's relentless distribution expansion into smaller towns.
- **Price growth (~4–5%):** Pidilite's brand strength and pricing power let it pass on raw-material inflation and premiumise its mix without losing customers — the hallmark of a real moat.

Add them and a **~12% revenue CAGR** over five years is a defensible base case for Pidilite — grounded in its history of low-teens growth, not pulled from thin air. *(All figures here are approximate FY24-scale numbers, rounded for teaching.)*

**Step 1 — the revenue forecast** (Year 0 base = ₹12,000 crore, growing 12%):

| Year | Revenue (₹ cr) |
|------|----------------|
| 0 (base) | 12,000 |
| 1 | 13,440 |
| 2 | 15,053 |
| 3 | 16,859 |
| 4 | 18,882 |
| 5 | 21,148 |

Notice what we *didn't* do: assume 20% growth to make the valuation "work." The growth rate flows from Pidilite's *story* — a dominant adhesives franchise compounding with Indian construction — exactly the narrative-first discipline from Session 12.1. If we later find the valuation only works at 18% growth, that's a signal to stop, not to keep typing.

One honest caveat we'll return to: 12% is a *conservative* base case, and the 5-year window is short for a business with Pidilite's long runway. Hold that thought — it becomes the punchline in Card 7.

**🧠 Remember:** Forecast revenue driver-based (volume + price), not as a single plucked number — Pidilite's ~12% CAGR comes from ~7–8% volume growth plus ~4–5% pricing power, and it must flow from the company's story, never be reverse-engineered to fit the price.

---

### CARD 3

> 🔍 **DEEP DIVE: Pidilite — Steps 2–4, from revenue to free cash flow.**

Now we turn revenue into FCFF, applying the exact build from Session 12.3. We'll use these driver assumptions (each defensible from Pidilite's FY24 profile, held flat for a clean base case): **EBITDA margin 23%**, **D&A ≈ 3.3% of revenue**, **capex ≈ 4.2% of revenue**, **working-capital increase ≈ 2.5% of revenue**, **tax 25%**.

*(A note on margins, per the moat: Pidilite's brand, distribution, and softening input costs genuinely support margin* expansion *over time — a real bull lever. We hold margins flat at 23% here for conservatism, and flag expansion as upside rather than bake it into the base case.)*

**Let's do Year 1 in full so every line is visible:**

| Line | Calculation | ₹ cr |
|------|-------------|------|
| Revenue | — | 13,440 |
| EBITDA (23%) | 13,440 × 0.23 | 3,091 |
| − D&A (3.3%) | 13,440 × 0.033 | (448) |
| **EBIT** | 3,091 − 448 | **2,643** |
| NOPAT (× 0.75) | 2,643 × (1 − 0.25) | 1,982 |
| + D&A (add back non-cash) | | +448 |
| − Capex (4.2%) | 13,440 × 0.042 | (560) |
| − ΔWorking Capital (2.5%) | 13,440 × 0.025 | (336) |
| **FCFF (Year 1)** | | **1,534** |

Because every driver is a flat percentage of revenue, FCFF works out to a steady **~11.4% of revenue** each year — so it grows in line with the 12% revenue CAGR. Applying that across the forecast:

| Year | Revenue (₹ cr) | FCFF (₹ cr) |
|------|----------------|-------------|
| 1 | 13,440 | 1,534 |
| 2 | 15,053 | 1,718 |
| 3 | 16,859 | 1,925 |
| 4 | 18,882 | 2,156 |
| 5 | 21,148 | 2,414 |

**The sanity check that matters:** Pidilite's FY24 net profit is ~₹1,750 crore, and our Year-1 FCFF is ~₹1,534 crore — a bit *below* profit, exactly as expected, because growth locks some cash into working capital and capex slightly exceeds depreciation. That's the "profit ≠ free cash" lesson from 12.3 in action, and the gap being *small* (not huge, like the steel case) tells you Pidilite is a high-quality, capital-light cash generator. If our FCFF had come out *wildly* different from profit, we'd stop and check our assumptions.

**🧠 Remember:** Build FCFF line by line — EBITDA, minus D&A to EBIT, taxed to NOPAT, then add back D&A and subtract capex and working capital — and for Pidilite this gives ~₹1,534 cr in Year 1, sensibly just below its ~₹1,750 cr profit because growth consumes some cash.

---

### CARD 4

> 💡 **CONCEPT: Pidilite — Step 5, computing the WACC.**

We have the cash flows; now we need the rate to discount them. Applying the CAPM and WACC machinery from Session 12.4 to Pidilite *(figures approximate)*:

**Cost of equity (CAPM):**
- Risk-free rate (Rf): **7%** (10-year G-Sec)
- Beta (β): **0.85** — Pidilite is a high-quality, relatively defensive consumer-facing business, so below-market volatility
- Equity risk premium (ERP): **6.5%**
- Ke = Rf + β × ERP = 7% + 0.85 × 6.5% = 7% + 5.5% = **12.5%**

**After-tax cost of debt:**
- Pre-tax Kd: **8%** (Pidilite is a strong credit, borrows cheaply)
- Post-tax: 8% × (1 − 0.25) = **6.0%**

**The weights (market values, never book):**
- Market cap (E): **~₹1,47,000 crore**
- Total debt (D): **~₹800 crore** (Pidilite is nearly debt-free)
- V = E + D = ₹1,47,800 crore → E/V = **99.5%**, D/V = **0.5%**

**Blend into WACC:**
WACC = (E/V × Ke) + (D/V × Kd × (1−t)) = (0.995 × 12.5%) + (0.005 × 6.0%) = 12.43% + 0.03% = **≈ 12.5%**

**Notice the pattern — again.** Pidilite's WACC (12.5%) is essentially *equal* to its cost of equity (12.5%), because the company is ~99.5% equity-funded. This is the same lesson Asian Paints taught in Session 12.4: **for a near-debt-free company, WACC ≈ Ke**, and the debt tax shield barely helps. Many of India's highest-quality compounders share this trait — they're so cash-rich they carry almost no debt, so their cost of capital is driven almost entirely by their cost of equity. We'll discount everything at **12.5%**.

And remember Session 12.4's warning made real: had we naïvely used Pidilite's *book* equity (a small fraction of its ₹1.47 lakh crore market cap) instead of market cap for the weights, we'd have wildly overstated the debt weight and understated the WACC — overvaluing the company through a pure weighting error.

**🧠 Remember:** Pidilite's WACC ≈ 12.5%, essentially equal to its cost of equity — because it's nearly debt-free, the rule "for a low-debt company, WACC ≈ Ke" holds again, and you must use its ₹1.47 lakh crore market cap (not book equity) for the weights.

---

### CARD 5

> 🔍 **DEEP DIVE: Pidilite — Steps 6–8, discounting, terminal value, and Enterprise Value.**

Now we discount every cash flow at the 12.5% WACC, add the terminal value, and sum to Enterprise Value.

**Step 6 — Discount each year's FCFF** (discount factor = 1 / 1.125ʸᵉᵃʳ):

| Year | FCFF (₹ cr) | Discount Factor @12.5% | PV of FCFF (₹ cr) |
|------|-------------|------------------------|--------------------|
| 1 | 1,534 | 0.8889 | 1,364 |
| 2 | 1,718 | 0.7901 | 1,358 |
| 3 | 1,925 | 0.7023 | 1,352 |
| 4 | 2,156 | 0.6243 | 1,346 |
| 5 | 2,414 | 0.5549 | 1,340 |
| | | **PV of explicit FCF (Yr 1–5)** | **6,759** |

*(Note how the present values are nearly flat at ~₹1,350 cr each — because FCFF grows ~12% while the discount factor shrinks ~12.5% per year, the two almost cancel. A neat illustration of growth racing against discounting.)*

**Step 7 — Terminal value** (Gordon Growth, g = 5%, WACC = 12.5%):
- FCFF in Year 6 = FCFF₅ × (1+g) = 2,414 × 1.05 = ₹2,535 cr
- TV at end of Year 5 = FCFF₆ / (WACC − g) = 2,535 / (0.125 − 0.05) = 2,535 / 0.075 = **₹33,803 cr**
- Discount the TV back to today: PV of TV = 33,803 × 0.5549 = **₹18,758 cr**

**Step 8 — Enterprise Value:**
EV = PV of explicit FCF + PV of TV = 6,759 + 18,758 = **≈ ₹25,518 crore**

And the now-familiar check: terminal value (₹18,758 cr) is **74%** of the total EV — squarely in the 60–80% range from Session 12.5. Even for a model where we forecast five years in detail, three-quarters of the value comes from the perpetuity. This is normal, and it's why our 5% terminal growth and 12.5% WACC carried more weight than any single year's revenue line.

So our DCF says Pidilite's *entire operating business* is worth roughly **₹25,500 crore** on these assumptions. Hold that number — because Pidilite's market cap is ₹1.47 *lakh* crore. Something interesting is about to happen in the next two cards.

**🧠 Remember:** Discount each FCFF at the WACC, add the discounted terminal value, and sum to Enterprise Value — for Pidilite that's ~₹6,759 cr (explicit) + ~₹18,758 cr (terminal, 74% of the total) = ~₹25,518 crore.

---

### CARD 6

> 📊 **VISUAL: The bridge from Enterprise Value to intrinsic value per share.**

*[Plain English description — what the student should imagine]*

Enterprise Value isn't what a shareholder owns — it's the value of the *whole business*, owed to both lenders and shareholders. To get to *per-share* value, you cross a bridge. Picture a left-to-right waterfall. Start with two stacked blocks — **PV of explicit FCF (₹6,759 cr)** and **PV of terminal value (₹18,758 cr)** — combining into the **Enterprise Value (₹25,518 cr)**. Then adjust for the balance sheet: Pidilite has more cash than debt (it's *net cash*), so instead of subtracting net debt you *add* the net cash back — **net debt is −₹1,500 cr (i.e. +₹1,500 cr net cash)** — lifting you to **Equity Value (₹27,018 cr)**. Finally, divide by the **50.8 crore shares** outstanding to land on **intrinsic value ≈ ₹532 per share**. Beside it, draw the **market price bar at ~₹2,900** — towering over the ₹532 intrinsic bar. The visual gap between the two is the whole story of this session.

The bridge in numbers: **Enterprise Value − Net Debt = Equity Value; Equity Value ÷ Shares = Value per Share.**
₹25,518 cr − (−₹1,500 cr) = ₹27,018 cr ÷ 50.8 cr shares = **₹532 per share.**

**AI Image Generation Prompt:**
> Create a widescreen 16:9 financial waterfall/bridge infographic, dark cosmic aesthetic — deep navy background (#02073D) with a faint starfield. Title in clean white sans-serif: "FROM ENTERPRISE VALUE TO VALUE PER SHARE — PIDILITE". Left to right, a bridge chart: bar 1 "PV of Explicit FCF ₹6,759 cr" (#246BFD blue), bar 2 stacked on top "PV of Terminal Value ₹18,758 cr" (#FF6AC1 pink, clearly the largest, labelled "74% of value"), combining into a tall bar "ENTERPRISE VALUE ₹25,518 cr" (#6A5CFF purple). Then a small upward floating bar "+ Net Cash ₹1,500 cr" (green tint) leading to "EQUITY VALUE ₹27,018 cr". Then a division icon "÷ 50.8 cr shares" leading to a final node "INTRINSIC VALUE ₹532 / share". To the right, separated by a gap, a tall solitary bar in muted grey labelled "MARKET PRICE ₹2,900" that visibly DWARFS the ₹532 intrinsic bar, with a dashed double-arrow between them labelled in #F5ECFF lavender "the gap = what the market believes". Captions in small JetBrains-Mono-style monospace. Style: clean, modern, high-contrast, generous spacing, Bricolage-Grotesque-style headings.

**Why this visual works:** It makes the EV-to-equity bridge concrete (including the net-*cash* nuance most students get wrong) and then delivers the session's gut-punch visually — a ₹532 intrinsic bar standing next to a ₹2,900 market bar. You don't need to be told the conclusion; you can *see* that the market is asking you to believe something your conservative model doesn't.

**🧠 Remember:** Enterprise Value − Net Debt = Equity Value, then ÷ shares = value per share — and for Pidilite, because it's net *cash*, you add ₹1,500 cr to reach ₹27,018 cr equity value, or **₹532 per share** versus a ~₹2,900 market price.

---

### CARD 7

> ⚠️ **THE MISTAKE: Trusting the DCF output as a verdict — and using too short a horizon.**

Our DCF values Pidilite at **₹532 per share**. The market price is **~₹2,900**. A naïve analyst draws the obvious conclusion: *"My model proves it — Pidilite is 80% overvalued. Strong Sell."* This is a serious mistake, and untangling it teaches the deepest lesson of the whole module.

**Why it's so easy to make:** the DCF produced a precise number, and precise numbers feel authoritative (the false-precision trap from 12.1). It's tempting to treat your model as reality and the market as wrong. But recall Session 12.1's discipline: *the market is rarely stupid.* When your model disagrees with the price by 5×, the first suspect is your model, not the market.

**What's actually going on — two intertwined causes.**
1. **The horizon is too short.** A 5-year explicit forecast plus a 5% terminal growth rate effectively assumes Pidilite stops compounding at low-teens rates *after year five* and grows at just 5% forever. But Pidilite has a *long competitive-advantage period* — its moat plausibly lets it grow 12%+ for 10–15 years, not 5. By truncating to 5 years, we lopped off a decade of high growth and handed it to the terminal value at a modest 5%. A fuller model with a longer high-growth phase (or a gradual "fade") would produce a materially higher intrinsic value. **Short-horizon DCFs systematically undervalue long-runway compounders.**
2. **The market is pricing that long runway.** The ₹2,900 price is the market's *implicit DCF* (Session 12.2), and it embeds far more years of strong compounding than our cautious model. The gap isn't proof the stock is overvalued — it's a precise statement of *what the market believes*: that Pidilite keeps compounding profitably for a very long time.

**The correct approach:** don't blurt "overvalued, sell." Instead, (a) recognise the horizon limitation and consider extending the high-growth period for a long-runway business; (b) reverse-engineer the price — *what growth and duration would justify ₹2,900?* — and judge whether that's plausible for Pidilite's moat; and (c) present a *range*, not a single number. The honest conclusion: "On conservative 5-year assumptions Pidilite looks expensive, but a vanilla short-horizon DCF understates elite compounders, and the market is clearly pricing a long, durable runway — so the real question is *how long can the moat last?*"

**The "what-if" test — and why it deepens the humility.** Push WACC up by just 1 point (12.5% → 13.5%) and re-run: Enterprise Value falls from ₹25,518 cr to **₹22,418 cr**, and intrinsic value drops from ₹532 to **₹471 per share — a ~12% swing from a single percentage point.** If a 1% WACC tweak moves the answer 12%, you should *never* treat the output as gospel. The DCF is a thinking tool, not an oracle.

**What the interviewer thinks:** a candidate who says "DCF says ₹532, so it's overvalued, sell" signals dangerous overconfidence. A candidate who says "my conservative DCF is below the price, but a 5-year horizon understates a long-runway compounder, and the market's pricing a longer moat — here's what you'd have to believe" signals real maturity.

**🧠 Remember:** A DCF output is a question, not a verdict — when Pidilite's ₹532 intrinsic sits far below the ₹2,900 price, the likely cause is a too-short horizon undervaluing a long-runway compounder plus the market pricing that runway, and a 1% WACC change swinging value ~12% proves you must present a range, never a single confident number.

---

### CARD 8

> 📝 **SESSION SUMMARY: The DCF Cheat Sheet**

```
🎯 The Big Idea:
A full DCF wires together free cash flow, WACC, and terminal value into one model
that produces an intrinsic value per share — and its real output is the LIST OF
ASSUMPTIONS the market is implicitly making, revealed by the gap to the price.

📋 THE 9-STEP DCF CHEAT SHEET (memorise this):
1. FORECAST REVENUE — driver-based: volume growth + price growth (Pidilite: ~12%).
2. PROJECT FCFF each year — EBITDA → −D&A → EBIT → ×(1−tax) = NOPAT → +D&A
   −Capex −ΔWorking Capital.
3. COMPUTE WACC — Ke = Rf + β·ERP; after-tax Kd = Kd·(1−t); weight by MARKET values.
   (Near-debt-free company → WACC ≈ Ke.)
4. DISCOUNT each FCFF — multiply by 1/(1+WACC)^year.
5. TERMINAL VALUE — TV = FCFₙ·(1+g)/(WACC−g), with g ≤ long-run GDP (~4–6% India).
6. DISCOUNT THE TV back to today — TV / (1+WACC)^n. (Don't forget this step.)
7. ENTERPRISE VALUE = Σ PV(explicit FCF) + PV(terminal value).
8. BRIDGE TO EQUITY — Equity Value = EV − Net Debt (add net cash if cash > debt);
   then ÷ shares = intrinsic value PER SHARE.
9. INTERPRET — compare to market price, run a WACC × g sensitivity, present a RANGE.

✅ PIDILITE — THE NUMBERS (base case):
• Revenue: ₹12,000 cr → ₹21,148 cr (12% CAGR, 5 yrs)
• FCFF: ₹1,534 cr (Yr1) → ₹2,414 cr (Yr5); PV of explicit FCF ≈ ₹6,759 cr
• WACC ≈ 12.5% (≈ Ke, near debt-free)
• Terminal Value ≈ ₹33,803 cr → PV ≈ ₹18,758 cr (74% of EV)
• Enterprise Value ≈ ₹25,518 cr → + net cash ₹1,500 cr = Equity ₹27,018 cr
• Intrinsic Value ≈ ₹532/share vs market ~₹2,900
• What-if (WACC +1% → 13.5%): value falls to ₹471/share (−12%)

⚡ The Analyst Move:
Don't read the per-share number as a verdict. Read the GAP to the price as the
market's implied assumptions, check whether your horizon is too short for a
long-runway business, reverse-engineer what the price requires, and present a range.

🔗 Connects to:
• Sessions 12.3 / 12.4 / 12.5 — the FCF, WACC, and TV you built now run as one machine.
• Session 12.2 — the price is the market's implicit DCF; the gap reveals its assumptions.
• Session 12.12 — next-level stress testing: sensitivity tables, scenarios, ranges.
• Session 12.15 — why selling Indian quality compounders on a short-horizon DCF has
  been one of the worst trades of the past decade.

💬 If an interviewer says "value this company for me," you now do exactly this — in order,
out loud, with every assumption defended, and a range at the end. That's the whole job.
```

---
---

## QUIZ (5 QUESTIONS)

---

**Q1. In the Pidilite DCF, revenue was forecast using a "driver-based" approach. What does this mean?**

A) Picking a single growth rate that makes the valuation match the market price
B) Breaking revenue growth into its underlying drivers — volume growth plus price growth — and forecasting each
C) Using last year's revenue as the forecast for every future year
D) Forecasting revenue based only on competitors' growth rates

**✅ Answer: B**

**Explanation:** Driver-based forecasting decomposes the top line into volume growth (more units sold) and price growth (pricing power and mix), because the two behave differently and reveal different parts of the company's story — for Pidilite, ~7–8% volume plus ~4–5% price gives ~12%. Option A is reverse-engineering, the cardinal sin the course warns against. C ignores growth entirely, and D outsources the forecast to peers rather than understanding the business's own drivers.

**Difficulty:** Easy

---

**Q2. A company has Year-1 NOPAT of ₹1,982 crore, D&A of ₹448 crore, capex of ₹560 crore, and a working-capital increase of ₹336 crore. What is its FCFF?**

A) ₹1,534 crore
B) ₹1,982 crore
C) ₹1,870 crore
D) ₹2,430 crore

**✅ Answer: A**

**Explanation:** FCFF = NOPAT + D&A − Capex − ΔWorking Capital = 1,982 + 448 − 560 − 336 = ₹1,534 crore (these are Pidilite's Year-1 figures). Option B stops at NOPAT, ignoring the cash adjustments. Option D (₹2,430) adds back D&A but forgets to subtract capex and working capital. Option C (₹1,870) subtracts capex but forgets the working-capital outflow. Every term must be applied.

**Difficulty:** Medium

---

**Q3. Pidilite's WACC works out to roughly 12.5%, almost identical to its cost of equity. Why?**

A) Because Pidilite has enormous debt that lowers its WACC
B) Because Pidilite is nearly debt-free, so the equity weight dominates and the tiny debt slice barely affects the blend
C) Because the cost of debt and cost of equity are always equal
D) Because WACC and cost of equity mean the same thing

**✅ Answer: B**

**Explanation:** When a company is ~99.5% equity-funded, like Pidilite, the equity weight overwhelms the WACC formula and the small, cheap debt slice is nearly irrelevant — so WACC collapses toward the cost of equity (~12.5%). This is the same rule Asian Paints illustrated in Session 12.4: for a near-debt-free company, WACC ≈ Ke. Option A is backwards; C is false (debt is almost always cheaper than equity); and D confuses two distinct concepts that merely happen to converge here.

**Difficulty:** Medium

---

**Q4. A DCF gives an Enterprise Value of ₹25,518 crore. The company has total debt of ₹800 crore, cash of ₹2,300 crore, and 50.8 crore shares outstanding. What is the intrinsic value per share?**

A) ₹532
B) ₹502
C) ₹473
D) ₹548

**✅ Answer: A**

**Explanation:** Net Debt = Total Debt − Cash = 800 − 2,300 = −₹1,500 crore (the company is *net cash*). Equity Value = EV − Net Debt = 25,518 − (−1,500) = ₹27,018 crore, and ÷ 50.8 crore shares = ₹532. Option B (₹502) forgets the balance-sheet adjustment entirely (EV ÷ shares). Option C (₹473) *subtracts* the net cash instead of adding it — the most common bridge error. Option D (₹548) adds gross cash but wrongly ignores the debt.

**Difficulty:** Hard

---

**Q5. Your DCF values Pidilite at ~₹532 per share, but it trades at ~₹2,900. What is the most defensible interpretation?**

A) The model proves Pidilite is 80% overvalued — issue a Strong Sell immediately
B) A short 5-year horizon understates a long-runway compounder, and the market is pricing a much longer, durable growth runway — so the real question is how long the moat lasts
C) The DCF is useless and should never be used for quality companies
D) The market price is definitely correct and the DCF is always wrong

**✅ Answer: B**

**Explanation:** When a model disagrees with the price by 5×, the disciplined first move is to suspect the model — and here two things are at work: a 5-year explicit window truncates Pidilite's long compounding runway (systematically undervaluing elite compounders), and the market price is an implicit DCF pricing a far longer runway. The honest output is a range and a question about moat durability, not a confident Sell. Option A is dangerous overconfidence; C overreacts (the DCF is still a valuable thinking tool); D blindly defers to the market, abandoning analysis.

**Difficulty:** Hard

---
---

## INTERVIEW QUESTIONS (3 QUESTIONS)

---

**IQ1. "Value this company for me — walk me through a full DCF from start to finish."**

*Difficulty:* Senior Analyst
*Type:* Technical
*Firm type likely to ask:* All

**💬 Strong answer:**
"I'd start by forecasting revenue driver-based — splitting growth into volume and price rather than picking one rate — over a five-to-ten-year explicit period, with the growth flowing from the company's competitive story. Then I'd build free cash flow each year: EBITDA, less depreciation to get EBIT, taxed to NOPAT, then add back depreciation and subtract capex and the change in working capital. Next I'd compute the WACC — cost of equity from CAPM, after-tax cost of debt, weighted by market values, not book. I'd discount each year's cash flow at that WACC, then calculate a terminal value using Gordon Growth with a long-term rate at or below nominal GDP, and discount that back too. Summing the discounted cash flows and terminal value gives Enterprise Value. I'd bridge to equity by subtracting net debt — or adding net cash if the company is cash-rich — and divide by shares for intrinsic value per share. Finally, I'd compare to the market price, run a WACC-versus-growth sensitivity table, and present a range rather than a single number, because a one-point WACC change can move the answer over ten percent."

**🚫 Weak answer trap:** Skipping the EV-to-equity bridge, forgetting to discount the terminal value, or presenting a single point estimate with no sensitivity — each signals an incomplete grasp of the full workflow.

**✅ What this question tests:** Whether you can execute the *entire* valuation chain cleanly, in order, and with the right caveats. This is the defining technical task of equity research and IB — and the most common interview request.

---

**IQ2. "How do you get from Enterprise Value to equity value per share?"**

*Difficulty:* Junior Analyst
*Type:* Technical
*Firm type likely to ask:* All

**💬 Strong answer:**
"Enterprise Value is the value of the whole business — it belongs to both lenders and shareholders — so to get to what equity holders own, I subtract net debt. Net debt is total debt minus cash and cash equivalents. If the company has more debt than cash, I subtract that positive net debt to get equity value. But if it's net cash — more cash than debt, like a lot of high-quality Indian companies — net debt is negative, so I effectively *add* the net cash back, which makes equity value *higher* than Enterprise Value. Then I divide equity value by the diluted share count to get intrinsic value per share. The net-cash case trips people up — they reflexively subtract and understate the value — so I'm careful about the sign. For something like Pidilite, which is net cash, the equity value comes out above the Enterprise Value."

**🚫 Weak answer trap:** Always subtracting net debt without recognising the net-cash case, or forgetting to adjust for cash at all — both produce a wrong per-share value, especially for cash-rich Indian companies.

**✅ What this question tests:** Whether you understand *why* the bridge exists (EV belongs to all capital providers) and can handle the net-cash nuance correctly. A favourite quick technical check.

---

**IQ3. "Your DCF on a high-quality company comes out well below its current market price. How do you interpret that?"**

*Difficulty:* Senior Analyst
*Type:* Judgment
*Firm type likely to ask:* Buy-side / Sell-side

**💬 Strong answer:**
"I wouldn't immediately conclude it's overvalued. When a model disagrees with the price by a wide margin, my first suspect is the model, because the market is rarely stupid. For a high-quality compounder, the most common culprit is too short a forecast horizon — a five-year explicit period plus a low terminal growth rate effectively assumes the business stops growing fast after year five, but a company with a durable moat might compound at high rates for ten or fifteen years. That truncation systematically undervalues elite compounders. So I'd consider extending the high-growth period or modelling a gradual fade. The other half of the answer is that the market price is itself an implicit DCF pricing a much longer runway, so the gap is really telling me what the market believes about moat durability. What I'd actually do is reverse-engineer the price — figure out what growth and duration justify it — and judge whether that's plausible for this specific business, then present a range. The gap is a question about how long the moat lasts, not automatically a sell signal."

**🚫 Weak answer trap:** "My DCF says it's overvalued, so I'd short it / put a Sell on it" — overconfidence in a short-horizon model is exactly how analysts get run over by quality compounders.

**✅ What this question tests:** Whether you understand the limitations of a vanilla DCF on long-runway businesses, can reverse-engineer the market's implied assumptions, and respond with humility and a range rather than a brittle point-estimate verdict. This is the analytical maturity buy-side and sell-side desks prize most.

---

*End of Session 12.6 — Building the Full DCF: Putting It All Together*
