# MODULE 12 — SESSION 12.7: Relative Valuation — The Art of Comparable Company Analysis

> *Two equally sharp analysts value the same stock using P/E "comparables." One concludes it's cheap; the other says it's expensive. Neither made an arithmetic error. They simply chose different peers — because in relative valuation, choosing the peer group isn't a step before the valuation. It IS the valuation.*

---

## SESSION OVERVIEW

**What you'll learn:** How practising analysts actually value most companies most of the time — not with a three-week DCF, but with comparable company analysis ("comps"). We'll establish what comps genuinely tell you (relative cheapness, not absolute worth), why EV-based multiples beat equity multiples when companies carry different debt, how to choose a *true* peer group (the hardest 50% of the job), why you almost always use forward rather than trailing multiples, how to read the spread across a peer set, when a premium or discount to the peer median is justified, and why Indian conglomerates and holding companies persistently trade below the sum of their parts.

**Why it matters:** Comps are the workhorse of equity research — fast, market-grounded, and what gets quoted in every research note and IPO. But they're also the easiest tool to fool yourself (and others) with, because the peer set and the multiple choice are so manipulable. Mastering comps as a *discipline* is what separates an analyst from someone averaging P/Es in a spreadsheet.

**Session difficulty:** ★★★☆☆ *(Conceptually lighter than DCF, but full of judgment traps — the rigour is in the choices, not the maths.)*

**Damodaran insight of the session:** A multiple is a story in shorthand — a compressed claim about growth, risk, and quality. Relative valuation feels objective because it uses market prices, but you're really making a deeply subjective choice about *which* companies are truly comparable, and that choice quietly determines your answer. In Indian terms: call Avenue Supermarts a peer of a struggling regional retailer and you'll "prove" DMart is wildly overvalued. Choose Trent and Vishal Mega Vmart instead and the story flips. Same maths, opposite conclusion.

---

## NOTES (8 CARDS)

---

### CARD 1

> 🎣 **HOOK — In comps, choosing the peers is the whole game.**

A DCF takes weeks. Relative valuation can take an afternoon. You find a handful of similar companies, look at the multiple they trade on — say a P/E of 25× — apply it to your company's earnings, and out pops a value. This speed is why **comps are the most-used valuation tool in the world**: every sell-side note, every IPO pricing, every quick "is this cheap?" call leans on them.

But here's the trap that makes comps simultaneously powerful and treacherous: **the entire answer depends on which companies you call "comparable."** Picture an analyst valuing Zomato. Compare it to other loss-making, high-growth food-and-quick-commerce platforms and it looks reasonably priced. Compare it to mature, profitable consumer companies trading at modest multiples and it looks insane. Compare it to a fast-growing US peer like DoorDash and you get yet another answer. *Nothing about the company changed.* Only the peer set did — and the peer set drove the conclusion.

This is why a wise mentor will tell you: **defining the peer group is 50% of relative valuation.** It's also the 50% that's easiest to manipulate, consciously or not. An analyst who wants to justify a "Buy" can quietly include expensive peers to make the stock look cheap by comparison; one who wants a "Sell" can do the reverse. The numbers stay honest while the conclusion gets cooked — entirely through peer selection.

So treat comps with the respect they deserve and the suspicion they require. Used rigorously — with a genuinely comparable peer set, the right multiple, and honest adjustments — relative valuation is a fast, market-anchored sanity check that complements your DCF. Used lazily — averaging whatever shares a sector label — it's a way to "prove" almost anything. This session is about doing it the rigorous way.

And remember the deepest point from Session 12.1: a multiple never gives you an *absolute* answer. It tells you whether a stock is cheap or expensive *relative to* its peers — which is useless, even dangerous, if the entire peer group is itself mispriced. Comps can tell you a stock is "cheap versus peers" while it's wildly overvalued in absolute terms. Cheap compared to *what* is the only question that matters.

**🧠 Remember:** Comps are fast and market-grounded but treacherous — the peer group you choose drives the answer, so defining a genuinely comparable peer set is 50% of the work, and a multiple only ever tells you cheap-or-expensive *relative to peers*, never absolute value.

---

### CARD 2

> 🔍 **DEEP DIVE: Enterprise Value multiples vs equity multiples — and why the difference matters.**

Multiples come in two families, and confusing them is a classic error. The distinction comes down to one rule from Session 12.3: *the numerator and denominator must belong to the same claimants.*

**Equity multiples** put an *equity* value on top and an *after-financing* number on the bottom. P/E (price ÷ EPS), P/B (price ÷ book value), P/Sales (market cap ÷ sales — loosely). The denominator (net income, book equity) is what's left *after* lenders are paid, so it belongs to shareholders. These multiples answer: "what is the *equity* worth relative to equity-level metrics?"

**Enterprise Value (EV) multiples** put the *whole firm's* value on top and a *pre-financing* number on the bottom. EV/EBITDA, EV/EBIT, EV/Sales. Here EV = market cap + net debt (the value of the *entire* business, owed to both lenders and shareholders), and the denominator (EBITDA, EBIT, sales) is measured *before* interest, so it also belongs to the whole firm. These answer: "what is the *whole business* worth relative to its operating performance?"

**Why EV multiples are usually better for cross-company comparison.** The killer advantage is that **EV/EBITDA is capital-structure-neutral.** Both EV and EBITDA sit at the firm level, *before* the financing decision, so two companies with very different debt loads can be compared apples-to-apples. P/E can't do this cleanly: a debt-heavy company's earnings are suppressed by interest expense, and its equity is a smaller, more leveraged slice — so its P/E reflects a tangle of operating performance *and* financing choices, not just how the business is doing. EV/EBITDA strips the financing out. It also sidesteps differences in depreciation policy and tax rates (it's pre-D&A and pre-tax), which is why sell-side desks lean on it so heavily.

**Where this really bites — capital-intensive sectors.** Compare telecom operators like Bharti Airtel against a debt-laden peer using P/E and you get garbage (one may even have negative earnings); compare them on EV/EBITDA and you get a sensible read on operating value. The rule of thumb: when companies differ meaningfully in leverage — telecom, infrastructure, cement, utilities — **default to EV-based multiples.** When you specifically want the *equity* angle (banks, where leverage *is* the business, or quick equity screens), P/E and P/B have their place. (We'll see in Session 12.9 why banks are the big exception.)

**The cardinal sin:** mixing the families — EV ÷ Net Income, or Price ÷ EBITDA. That pairs a firm-level number with an equity-level one (or vice versa), comparing claims that don't match. It's the multiples equivalent of discounting FCFF at the cost of equity. Keep the numerator and denominator on the same side of the debt.

**🧠 Remember:** Equity multiples (P/E, P/B) pair equity value with after-interest metrics; EV multiples (EV/EBITDA) pair whole-firm value with pre-interest metrics — and EV/EBITDA is preferred for comparing companies because it's neutral to capital structure, never mix the two families.

---

### CARD 3

> 🌍 **REAL WORLD: Zomato vs Swiggy — what actually makes a company a "peer."**

**Companies:** Zomato (listed parent rebranded to Eternal Ltd, NSE: ETERNAL) and Swiggy Ltd (NSE: SWIGGY, listed November 2024) · Sector: Online food delivery + quick commerce

If the peer group is 50% of relative valuation, what *makes* a true peer? It is emphatically **not** "same sector on the stock screener." A genuine comparable shares the economic DNA of the business. The test runs across at least five dimensions:

1. **Business model.** Zomato and Swiggy are near-perfect peers because they do the *same thing*: run two-sided platforms connecting restaurants and consumers (food delivery) and operate quick-commerce arms (Blinkit for Zomato, Instamart for Swiggy). Same unit economics, same structure. Now contrast with **Jubilant FoodWorks** (Domino's India) — also "food," but a completely different model: it *operates* restaurants, owns kitchens, carries inventory. Zomato is a platform; Jubilant is an operator. *Not* comparable, despite the shared "food" label.
2. **Geography.** Both Zomato and Swiggy are India-centric, facing the same consumer behaviour, regulations, and competitive intensity. **DoorDash** runs the identical *model* but in the US — different growth rates, margins, regulation, and market maturity. It's a useful *reference point*, not a direct comp.
3. **Growth stage.** Both are in the same phase — scaling rapidly, approaching or just past profitability in food delivery while investing heavily in quick commerce. A mature, slow-growing business isn't a peer to a hyper-growth one, even in the same industry.
4. **Margins and profitability path.** Their contribution margins and the trajectory toward profitability are broadly similar, which makes their multiples genuinely comparable.
5. **Competitive position.** They are *direct duopolists* fighting for the same customers — about as close as Indian peers get.

So for Zomato, **Swiggy is the cleanest comparable in the Indian market**, and vice versa. And because both were loss-making or thin on profit as they scaled, you can't compare them on P/E (no meaningful earnings) — you use **EV/Sales** or **EV/GOV** (gross order value), exactly the lesson from Session 12.1.

**The trap to avoid:** lumping by sector label. Zomato and Hindustan Unilever both sit in "consumer," but they are *not* peers — utterly different business models, growth, and economics. Meanwhile, two companies the screener files under different "sectors" might be far closer peers than two filed under the same one. **Define peers by business reality, not by the label.**

**The lesson for every sector:** before you compute a single multiple, build the peer set deliberately — same model, geography, growth stage, margins, and competitive position. A sloppy peer group produces a precise, confident, *wrong* answer.

**🧠 Remember:** A true peer shares business model, geography, growth stage, margins, and competitive position — Zomato and Swiggy are near-perfect comps while Jubilant FoodWorks (an operator, not a platform) and DoorDash (US, not India) are not, so build peer sets by business reality, never by sector label.

---

### CARD 4

> 💡 **CONCEPT: Forward vs trailing multiples — why analysts price the future, not the past.**

When you see "P/E of 25×," there's a hidden question: 25× *which* earnings — last year's or next year's? The answer matters enormously.

**Trailing (TTM/LTM) multiple:** based on the *last twelve months'* actual, reported earnings. Backward-looking. It's factual (no estimates involved) but it values a company on a year that's already gone.

**Forward (NTM — Next Twelve Months) multiple:** based on *estimated* earnings for the *next* twelve months. Forward-looking. NTM P/E = current price ÷ projected next-year EPS.

**Why analysts almost always use forward multiples.** Markets price the *future*. A stock's value reflects the cash it will generate *from here on*, not what it earned last year. This matters most for growing companies: a fast grower might look terrifyingly expensive on trailing earnings but perfectly reasonable on next year's. Concretely — a stock at ₹100 with trailing EPS of ₹4 trades at a **25× trailing** P/E. If its earnings are expected to rise to ₹5 next year, its **forward** P/E is just 100 ÷ 5 = **20×**. Same price, but the forward multiple correctly shows the stock is cheaper once you account for the growth the market is paying for. Comparing companies on *trailing* multiples penalises the fast growers and flatters the stagnant ones; forward multiples level the field by looking ahead.

**The Indian convention.** Sell-side reports quote forward multiples relentlessly — you'll see "FY26E P/E" and "FY27E EV/EBITDA," using consensus estimates for the coming fiscal years. When a Motilal Oswal note says Infosys trades at "22× FY26E," that's a forward multiple on next year's estimated earnings.

**The honest caveat.** A forward multiple is only as good as the estimates behind it. *Garbage estimates in, garbage multiple out.* For cyclical businesses near a peak, forward earnings can be wildly optimistic, making the forward P/E look deceptively low right before earnings collapse — a classic value trap. So use forward multiples, but interrogate the estimates: are they realistic, or is the "cheap" forward P/E resting on a hockey-stick forecast that won't materialise?

**🧠 Remember:** Use forward (NTM) multiples, not trailing ones, because markets price future earnings — a fast grower can look expensive on last year's earnings but reasonable on next year's — but always check the forecasts behind a forward multiple, since optimistic estimates make a stock look deceptively cheap.

---

### CARD 5

> 📊 **VISUAL: Reading the spread — range, median, mean, and the meaningful outlier.**

*[Plain English description — what the student should imagine]*

Once you have a clean peer set, you don't just take the average and stop. You read the *spread*. Picture a comps table for India's large-cap IT sector, then a horizontal "football field" line showing where the peers cluster. The table below shows illustrative **forward P/E** multiples *(approximate, for teaching)*:

| Company (NSE) | Forward P/E (×) |
|---|---|
| LTIMindtree | 30 |
| Tech Mahindra | 26 |
| TCS | 25 |
| Infosys | 24 |
| HCLTech | 23 |
| Wipro | 20 |

- **Range:** 20× (Wipro) to 30× (LTIMindtree) — a wide spread that itself tells a story about quality and growth differences.
- **Mean (average):** ~24.7×
- **Median (middle):** ~24.5× (average of the two central values, 24 and 25)

**Why the median usually beats the mean.** The mean is dragged around by extremes — one richly valued outlier (LTIMindtree at 30×) pulls the average up and can misrepresent the "typical" peer. The **median is robust to outliers**, so analysts lean on it as the central benchmark. Look at *both*: the median anchors the typical multiple, and the range reveals how much dispersion (and disagreement) exists in the sector.

**When the outlier is the right benchmark.** Here's the subtlety most students miss: you don't blindly apply the median to your company. You apply the multiple of the peers *most similar to your target*. If you're valuing a fast-growing mid-cap IT firm, LTIMindtree's 30× — the "outlier" — may be the *correct* comp, not the large-cap median, because the outlier is the closest match. The benchmark is the *most comparable* peer, not a mechanical average of everyone. An outlier isn't noise to discard; sometimes it's the signal.

**AI Image Generation Prompt:**
> Create a widescreen 16:9 financial infographic, dark cosmic aesthetic — deep navy background (#02073D) with a faint starfield. Title in clean white sans-serif: "READING THE COMPS SPREAD — INDIAN IT (Forward P/E)". Render a horizontal "football field" number line from 18× to 32×. Plot six labelled dots in #246BFD blue: Wipro 20, HCLTech 23, Infosys 24, TCS 25, Tech Mahindra 26, LTIMindtree 30. Draw a vertical #6A5CFF purple line at the MEDIAN (24.5×) labelled "MEDIAN — the robust benchmark", and a thinner dashed #FF6AC1 pink line at the MEAN (24.7×) labelled "MEAN — pulled by outliers". Shade a soft band between 23× and 26× labelled "peer cluster". Highlight the LTIMindtree dot (30×) with a pink glow and a callout: "outlier — but the RIGHT comp for a high-growth mid-cap". On the right, a small box: "Range 20–30×  ·  Median 24.5×  ·  Spread tells the quality story". Captions in JetBrains-Mono-style monospace. Style: clean, modern, high-contrast, generous spacing, Bricolage-Grotesque-style headings.

**Why this visual works:** It turns a list of numbers into a *distribution* you can see — the cluster, the median anchor, and the outlier — making concrete why the median beats the mean and why the "outlier" can be the correct benchmark for a similar company. Analysts present comps exactly this way (the "football field") in real research, so the student learns the actual professional exhibit.

**🧠 Remember:** Don't just average a peer set — read the spread: use the median (robust to outliers) as the central benchmark, the range to gauge dispersion, and remember that the most *comparable* peer (even an outlier) is the right benchmark for your target, not a mechanical mean.

---

### CARD 6

> 💡 **CONCEPT: Premiums and discounts — when a company deserves more (or less) than the peer median.**

The peer median is a *starting point*, not a verdict. The real analytical work is deciding whether your company deserves to trade *above* or *below* it — and *why*. A company is not entitled to the median; it earns a premium or a discount based on fundamentals.

**When a premium (trading above the peer median) is justified:**
- **Superior growth** — faster, more durable revenue and earnings growth than peers.
- **Higher returns** — better ROCE / ROE, meaning it creates more value per rupee invested (recall the ROCE-vs-WACC test from 12.4).
- **Stronger balance sheet** — net cash vs peers' debt, lower financial risk.
- **Dominant market position / scale** — a leader with pricing power and a wider moat.
- **Pricing power** — the ability to raise prices and protect margins.
- **Higher earnings quality** — cleaner accounting, lower volatility, better cash conversion, stronger governance and capital allocation.

**When a discount (trading below the peer median) is justified:** the mirror image — slower growth, lower returns, a weaker balance sheet, cyclicality, smaller scale, a commoditised offering, governance concerns, or a regulatory/political overhang.

**The discipline that turns comps into analysis.** Don't say "Infosys should trade at the 24.5× sector median." Ask: *is Infosys better or worse than the average peer* on growth, returns, balance sheet, and moat? TCS, for instance, justifiably trades at a premium to the IT median because of its scale, consistency, margins, and cash generation — the market rightly pays up for quality. A perennial laggard trades below for the opposite reasons. The multiple you finally apply to your company is the median *adjusted* for where it sits on these fundamental dimensions.

This is the bridge back to Session 12.1: a multiple is a *story* about growth, risk, and quality. Premium and discount analysis is simply *decoding* that story and asking whether your company's specific story warrants paying more or less than the typical peer. Get this right and you've moved from "averaging P/Es" to genuine valuation judgment — which is exactly what gets you hired.

**🧠 Remember:** The peer median is a starting point, not a target — a company earns a *premium* for superior growth, higher ROCE, a stronger balance sheet, dominance, and pricing power, and a *discount* for the opposite, so your job is to justify where your specific company sits relative to peers, not to apply the median blindly.

---

### CARD 7

> ⚠️ **THE MISTAKE: Treating the sum-of-parts as the price — ignoring the conglomerate discount.**

Here's a mistake that catches even experienced analysts. You value a diversified company by adding up its pieces — segment by segment, each at an appropriate multiple — arrive at a "sum-of-the-parts" (SOTP) value, and present *that* as the target price, expecting the market to agree. It almost never does. **Conglomerates and holding companies persistently trade at a *discount* to their SOTP**, and assuming the market will pay the full sum is a recipe for a wrong call.

**Why it's so easy to make:** the SOTP arithmetic is clean and compelling — "this division is worth ₹X, that one ₹Y, so the whole is worth ₹X+Y." It *feels* like you've found hidden value the market is missing. But the discount exists for real, structural reasons, not market stupidity.

**Why the discount exists.**
- **Tax leakage:** to actually unlock the parts, the company would have to sell stakes and pay capital-gains tax — so the realisable value is less than the gross sum.
- **Capital misallocation / agency risk:** conglomerate management might divert cash from strong businesses to prop up weak ones; investors discount for that risk.
- **Lack of a pure-play premium:** investors who want exposure to *one* business prefer a focused pure-play; a conglomerate forces them to own divisions they don't want, so they pay less.
- **Complexity and opacity:** diversified structures are harder to analyse, and the market charges a "confusion discount."

**Indian examples.**
- **Bajaj Holdings & Investment (NSE: BAJAJHLDNG):** essentially a holding company owning large stakes in Bajaj Auto and Bajaj Finserv. The market value of those stakes is *far higher* than Bajaj Holdings' own market cap — it has historically traded at roughly a **40–50% discount** to the value of its holdings. A textbook holding-company discount.
- **Tata Motors (NSE: TATAMOTORS):** three very different businesses under one roof — Jaguar Land Rover (global luxury), India commercial vehicles, and India passenger vehicles (plus EVs) — which long traded at a discount to their SOTP. Notably, Tata Motors moved to **demerge its commercial-vehicle and passenger-vehicle (including JLR) businesses into two separately listed entities** — a textbook corporate action *designed* to unlock the conglomerate discount by letting the market value each pure-play on its own.

**The correct approach:** compute the SOTP as the *gross* value, then apply a sensible holding-company/conglomerate discount (commonly **20–50%**, justified case by case), and recognise the discount can persist for *years* — don't assume it closes on its own. But also watch for **catalysts** that could unlock it: a demerger, spin-off, or restructuring (like Tata Motors'). A *narrowing* discount is itself a legitimate investment thesis. (We build full SOTP valuation in Session 12.11.)

**What the interviewer thinks:** a candidate who presents a raw SOTP as the target signals *"doesn't understand why conglomerates trade where they do."* A candidate who says "I'd apply a holding-company discount and look for a value-unlocking catalyst" signals real market awareness.

**🧠 Remember:** Conglomerates and holding companies trade at a persistent discount to their sum-of-parts (Bajaj Holdings ~40–50% below its stakes; Tata Motors demerging to unlock it) due to tax leakage, agency risk, and the lack of a pure-play premium — so never present the raw SOTP as the price; apply a discount and hunt for catalysts that close it.

---

### CARD 8

> 📝 **SESSION SUMMARY: What You Now Know**

```
🎯 The Big Idea:
Relative valuation tells you whether a stock is cheap or expensive COMPARED TO
similar businesses — never absolute worth. It's fast and market-grounded, but the
peer group you choose IS the valuation, so the rigour is all in the choices.

✅ 5 Things You Now Know:
1. Comps give RELATIVE, not absolute, value — "cheap vs peers" can still mean
   overvalued if the whole peer group is mispriced. Defining peers is 50% of the work.
2. EV multiples (EV/EBITDA) are capital-structure-neutral and best for comparing
   companies with different debt; equity multiples (P/E, P/B) mix in financing effects.
   Never mix the families (no EV/Net Income, no Price/EBITDA).
3. A true peer matches business model, geography, growth stage, margins, and
   competitive position — Zomato/Swiggy are peers; Zomato/HUL are not. Reality, not label.
4. Use FORWARD (NTM) multiples, not trailing — markets price the future — but
   interrogate the estimates behind them (optimistic forecasts = value traps).
5. The peer median is a starting point: justify a PREMIUM (superior growth, ROCE,
   balance sheet, dominance) or DISCOUNT, and remember conglomerates/holding companies
   trade BELOW their sum-of-parts.

⚡ The Analyst Move:
A textbook student averages the peer P/Es and applies it. A working analyst builds a
deliberate peer set, uses forward EV multiples, reads the spread (median over mean),
benchmarks against the MOST comparable peer, and justifies a premium or discount from
fundamentals — then cross-checks against the DCF.

🔗 Connects to:
• Session 12.1 — a multiple is a story; comps are about decoding "compared to what?"
• Session 12.3 — the EV-vs-equity rule mirrors FCFF (firm) vs FCFE (equity).
• Session 12.8 — next we decode each multiple (P/E, EV/EBITDA, P/B, EV/Sales...) in depth.
• Session 12.11 — full Sum-of-Parts valuation, and the conglomerate discount in detail.

💬 If someone asks you "how does relative valuation work?" at an interview, say:
"Relative valuation values a company against comparable peers using multiples — it tells
me whether a stock is cheap or expensive relative to similar businesses, not its absolute
worth. The hardest and most important part is choosing a genuine peer set — same business
model, geography, growth stage, and margins — because the peers drive the answer. I'd
default to EV-based multiples like EV/EBITDA since they're neutral to capital structure,
use forward estimates because markets price the future, look at the peer median rather
than the mean, and then justify a premium or discount based on the company's growth,
returns, and balance sheet versus peers. And I'd always cross-check it against a DCF."
```

---

## QUIZ (5 QUESTIONS)

---

**Q1. What does a relative valuation (comps) analysis actually tell you?**

A) The exact intrinsic value of a company
B) Whether a stock is cheap or expensive relative to similar companies — not its absolute worth
C) The company's future cash flows
D) Whether the company will go bankrupt

**✅ Answer: B**

**Explanation:** Comps measure a company *against its peers* — they reveal relative cheapness or expensiveness, not a standalone intrinsic value. This is why comps are dangerous when the entire peer group is mispriced: a stock can look "cheap versus peers" while being overvalued in absolute terms. Option A describes a DCF's aim (intrinsic value), C is what cash-flow analysis provides, and D is a solvency question unrelated to relative valuation.

**Difficulty:** Easy

---

**Q2. Why is EV/EBITDA generally preferred over P/E when comparing companies that have very different levels of debt?**

A) Because EV/EBITDA is always a smaller number
B) Because EV/EBITDA is capital-structure-neutral — both EV and EBITDA sit at the firm level before financing — while P/E mixes operating performance with financing effects
C) Because P/E cannot be calculated for large companies
D) Because EBITDA is the same as net profit

**✅ Answer: B**

**Explanation:** EBITDA is measured before interest and EV captures the whole firm (equity + debt), so EV/EBITDA compares companies on an operating basis regardless of how each is financed. P/E, by contrast, uses after-interest earnings and the equity slice alone — both distorted by leverage — so it tangles operating results with financing choices. Option A is irrelevant (size of the number doesn't matter), C is false, and D is wrong (EBITDA is pre-interest, pre-tax, pre-D&A, far above net profit).

**Difficulty:** Medium

---

**Q3. You are building a peer group to value Zomato (online food delivery + quick commerce). Which is the single best comparable?**

A) Hindustan Unilever — also in the consumer sector
B) Swiggy — same business model, geography, growth stage, and competitive position
C) Jubilant FoodWorks (Domino's India) — also in food
D) Tata Consultancy Services — also a large listed company

**✅ Answer: B**

**Explanation:** Swiggy shares Zomato's economic DNA — the same two-sided platform model, the same Indian market, a similar growth stage and margin path, and direct duopoly competition — making it the cleanest comparable. HUL is "consumer" by label but a completely different business; Jubilant *operates* restaurants (an operator, not a platform); and TCS shares nothing but a stock listing. The lesson: peers are defined by business reality, not by sector tags or size.

**Difficulty:** Medium

---

**Q4. A stock trades at ₹100. Its trailing (last twelve months) EPS is ₹4, and its expected next-twelve-months (NTM) EPS is ₹5. What is its forward P/E, and why do analysts prefer it?**

A) 25×, because trailing earnings are more reliable
B) 20×, because forward multiples reflect the future earnings that markets actually price
C) 16×, because you add the two EPS figures
D) 12.5×, because you average the two EPS figures

**✅ Answer: B**

**Explanation:** Forward P/E = price ÷ NTM EPS = 100 ÷ 5 = 20×. Analysts prefer forward multiples because a stock's value reflects the cash it will generate *going forward*, not what it earned last year — so a growing company that looks expensive at 25× trailing is more reasonably priced at 20× forward. Option A computes the trailing multiple (100 ÷ 4 = 25×) and gives the wrong rationale; C and D invent nonsensical EPS arithmetic. (Caveat: forward multiples are only as good as the estimates behind them.)

**Difficulty:** Medium

---

**Q5. Bajaj Holdings & Investment (NSE: BAJAJHLDNG) owns large stakes in Bajaj Auto and Bajaj Finserv worth far more than its own market capitalisation, yet it trades at roughly a 40–50% discount to the value of those holdings. What is the best explanation?**

A) The market has made an obvious error that an analyst should arbitrage immediately
B) Holding companies trade at a persistent discount to their sum-of-parts due to tax leakage on unlocking value, agency/capital-allocation risk, and the lack of a pure-play premium
C) Bajaj Holdings' stakes are actually worthless
D) The discount means the underlying companies are overvalued

**✅ Answer: B**

**Explanation:** The holding-company (conglomerate) discount is real and structural: to monetise the stakes you'd pay capital-gains tax, management could misallocate the cash, and investors who want one business must take the whole holding structure — so they pay less than the raw sum. It is *not* an obvious arbitrage (A), since the discount can persist for years; the stakes are genuinely valuable (C is false); and the discount reflects the holding structure, not overvaluation of the underlying companies (D). An analyst applies a discount to the SOTP and watches for catalysts (like a demerger) that could narrow it.

**Difficulty:** Hard

---
---

## INTERVIEW QUESTIONS (3 QUESTIONS)

---

**IQ1. "What's the difference between EV/EBITDA and P/E, and when would you use each?"**

*Difficulty:* Junior Analyst
*Type:* Technical
*Firm type likely to ask:* All

**💬 Strong answer:**
"P/E is an equity multiple — price over earnings per share — so the numerator is the equity value and the denominator is after-interest profit that belongs to shareholders. EV/EBITDA is an enterprise multiple — Enterprise Value, which is market cap plus net debt, over EBITDA, which is a pre-interest operating number that belongs to the whole firm. The key reason I'd usually prefer EV/EBITDA for comparing companies is that it's neutral to capital structure: because both EV and EBITDA are measured before financing, I can compare a debt-heavy company and a debt-free one on an apples-to-apples operating basis. P/E gets distorted by leverage, since a levered company's earnings are suppressed by interest and its equity is a smaller, riskier slice. So for capital-intensive sectors like telecom or infrastructure, where debt levels vary a lot, I'd default to EV/EBITDA. P/E and P/B still have their place — especially for banks, where leverage is the business itself. The one thing I'd never do is mix the families, like EV over net income, because that pairs a firm-level number with an equity-level one."

**🚫 Weak answer trap:** Saying "EV/EBITDA is just better" without explaining *why* (capital-structure neutrality), or mixing the families (e.g., suggesting EV/Net Income) — both reveal a shallow grasp.

**✅ What this question tests:** Whether you understand that the numerator and denominator must belong to the same claimants, and why capital-structure neutrality makes EV multiples the cross-company default. A guaranteed comps question.

---

**IQ2. "How do you select a peer group for a relative valuation?"**

*Difficulty:* Senior Analyst
*Type:* Judgment
*Firm type likely to ask:* Buy-side / Sell-side

**💬 Strong answer:**
"I'd build it deliberately, because the peer group basically determines the answer, so it's the most important judgment in the whole exercise. I don't just take whatever shares a sector label — I look for companies with the same economic DNA across a few dimensions: the same business model, the same geography, a similar growth stage, comparable margins and profitability, and a similar competitive position. For example, Zomato and Swiggy are genuine peers — same two-sided platform model, same Indian market, same growth phase. But Zomato and a restaurant operator like Jubilant FoodWorks aren't, even though both are 'food,' because one is a platform and the other owns kitchens and inventory. And a US peer like DoorDash is a useful reference but not a direct comp, because the geography, growth, and margins differ. I'd also be honest that peer selection is easy to abuse — you can make a stock look cheap or expensive by cherry-picking peers — so I try to choose the set I'd defend to a sceptic, and I'd flag where the comparables are imperfect rather than pretend they're clean."

**🚫 Weak answer trap:** "I'd use companies in the same sector" — far too crude. Sector labels group businesses that aren't economically comparable and miss peers filed under other sectors.

**✅ What this question tests:** Whether you grasp that peer selection *is* the valuation, can articulate the dimensions of true comparability, and are aware of how manipulable the peer set is. This separates analysts from spreadsheet-averagers.

---

**IQ3. "When is it justified for a company to trade at a premium to its peer group?"**

*Difficulty:* Senior Analyst
*Type:* Conceptual / Judgment
*Firm type likely to ask:* All

**💬 Strong answer:**
"A company earns a premium when its fundamentals are genuinely better than the average peer. The big drivers are superior growth — faster and more durable revenue and earnings growth; higher returns on capital, meaning a better ROCE or ROE that creates more value per rupee invested; a stronger balance sheet, like net cash versus peers carrying debt; a dominant market position with real pricing power and a wider moat; and higher earnings quality — cleaner accounting, lower volatility, better cash conversion, and good governance. TCS, for instance, justifiably trades above the Indian IT median because of its scale, consistency, margins, and cash generation — the market is rightly paying for quality. The discipline I'd apply is to never just slap the peer median on a company; I'd ask whether it's better or worse than the average peer on each of these dimensions and adjust the multiple up or down accordingly. A premium has to be *earned* by fundamentals — it's not a given, and a high multiple without the quality to back it is just an expensive stock."

**🚫 Weak answer trap:** "Because it's a good company" or "because it's a market leader" without the specific fundamental drivers (growth, returns, balance sheet, moat) — vague and unconvincing.

**✅ What this question tests:** Whether you can connect a multiple back to the fundamentals that justify it — decoding the "story inside the multiple" — rather than applying the peer median mechanically. This is the judgment that turns comps into real analysis.

---

*End of Session 12.7 — Relative Valuation: The Art of Comparable Company Analysis*

# MODULE 12 — SESSION 12.8: Multiples Decoded — P/E, EV/EBITDA, P/B, EV/Sales, and the Rest

> *State Bank of India trades at a P/E of about 8×. Kotak Mahindra Bank trades at roughly three times that. A beginner sees this and concludes SBI is "three times cheaper" — buy it. That conclusion is wrong, and understanding exactly why it's wrong is the difference between reading a number and reading a business.*

---

## SESSION OVERVIEW

**What you'll learn:** Every major valuation multiple — not as a formula, but as a *story* with a specific job. We'll decode P/E (the most used and most abused) and its growth-adjusted cousin PEG; EV/EBITDA (the sell-side favourite) and why depreciation-heavy businesses need EV/EBIT instead; P/B (why it's the primary tool for banks, and useless for asset-light firms); EV/Sales (the loss-maker's multiple); and the specialists — Dividend Yield and EV/Invested Capital. For each: what it measures, when to use it, when it *lies*, the typical Indian range, and a real example. The recurring theme: every multiple has a domain where it works and a domain where it fools you.

**Why it matters:** Multiples are the everyday language of equity research — quoted in every note, every pitch, every screen. But each one breaks in specific situations, and applying the wrong multiple to the wrong business is how analysts get fooled (and fool others). Knowing which tool fits which business — and each tool's failure mode — is core craft.

**Session difficulty:** ★★★★☆ *(Breadth over depth — eight tools to master, each with a trap. The skill is matching, not memorising.)*

**Damodaran insight of the session:** No multiple is "right" or "wrong" — each is a lens that brings some things into focus and blurs others. The mistake is treating a multiple as a verdict instead of a question: a low P/E doesn't mean "cheap," it means "the market expects low growth or high risk — is it correct?" In Indian terms: SBI's 8× isn't a discount waiting to be claimed; it's the market's considered view on a slower-growing, government-controlled bank, and your job is to judge whether that view is right, not to assume it's a free lunch.

---

## NOTES (8 CARDS)

---

### CARD 1

> 🎣 **HOOK — The P/E trap: why "cheaper" SBI isn't actually cheaper than Kotak.**

Here's a comparison that exposes the most dangerous misuse of any multiple in Indian markets. **State Bank of India** trades at a P/E of roughly **8×**. **Kotak Mahindra Bank** trades at around **22×** (and has historically commanded 30×+). The naive read writes itself: *"Kotak costs nearly three times as much per rupee of profit. SBI is far cheaper. Obviously, buy SBI."*

This is the **P/E trap**, and it has cost retail investors fortunes. The error is treating P/E as a standalone measure of cheapness, when it's actually a *compressed story* about three things — and the two banks have completely different stories:

1. **Growth.** A private bank like Kotak has historically grown its loan book and profits faster than a giant PSU like SBI. Faster growth justifies a higher multiple, because you're buying a bigger future, not just today's earnings.
2. **Return quality (ROE) and asset quality.** Kotak has long earned strong, consistent returns on equity with a clean loan book and a low-cost deposit franchise. PSU banks have historically carried higher bad loans (NPAs), lumpier earnings, and the overhang of government ownership and policy interference. The market pays up for clean, predictable, high-quality earnings.
3. **Risk and durability.** Government control, political lending pressures, and capital constraints make a PSU bank's future less certain. Lower certainty = lower multiple.

So the market isn't being irrational — it's making a *considered judgment*. SBI's 8× isn't a hidden bargain; it's the market's verdict on a slower-growing, higher-risk, government-controlled bank. Kotak's 22× is what investors willingly pay for growth, quality, and consistency. The P/E gap is the *story*, not a mispricing.

The lesson — which echoes Sessions 12.1 and 12.7 — is absolute: **a multiple is meaningless without its context.** You cannot compare two companies' P/E ratios without first understanding their growth, returns, and risk. "Low P/E" is not a synonym for "cheap." This session decodes every major multiple precisely so you stop reading numbers and start reading the businesses behind them.

**🧠 Remember:** SBI's ~8× P/E isn't "cheaper" than Kotak's ~22× — the gap encodes differences in growth, return quality (ROE/asset quality), and risk, so a P/E is a story about a business, never a standalone signal of cheapness.

---

### CARD 2

> 🔍 **DEEP DIVE: P/E and PEG — the most-used multiple, and the growth fix it needs.**

**P/E (Price-to-Earnings) = Price ÷ EPS = Market Cap ÷ Net Profit.**

**What it measures (plain English):** how many rupees you pay for each rupee of *annual* profit. A P/E of 25× means you pay ₹25 for ₹1 of yearly earnings — loosely, ~25 years of *current* profit to earn back your money (ignoring growth). It's the market's price tag on a company's earnings. For example, TCS at ~25× means investors pay ₹25 for every ₹1 of TCS's annual profit — a premium they accept for its quality and consistency.

**When to use it:** profitable companies with relatively *stable* earnings — consumer, IT, pharma, asset-light businesses with consistent profits — and for comparing genuinely similar companies (same sector, similar growth and risk).

**When it lies (failure modes):** (1) **Loss-making companies** — a negative P/E is meaningless. (2) **Cyclicals at peak or trough** — at a cyclical *peak*, earnings are inflated, so the P/E looks deceptively *low*, luring you into a value trap right before profits collapse; at a trough, the opposite. (3) **Different capital structures** — leverage distorts EPS, so P/E isn't clean across companies with very different debt. (4) **One-off items** — a single large gain or charge warps the multiple.

**Indian range:** large-caps typically **15–30×**; quality FMCG **40–60×**; IT **20–28×**; PSUs and cyclicals **6–12×**. The Nifty's long-run average sits around 18–22×.

**The fatal blind spot — and PEG, the fix.** P/E's biggest weakness is that it *ignores growth entirely*. A 30× P/E looks expensive until you learn the company grows earnings at 30% a year. Peter Lynch's solution: the **PEG ratio = P/E ÷ earnings growth rate (%).** It normalises the multiple by growth:
- **PEG < 1** → potentially undervalued (you're paying less than the growth rate).
- **PEG ≈ 1** → fairly valued (Lynch's benchmark for a reasonable price).
- **PEG > 1** → expensive relative to growth; **PEG > 2** → likely overvalued.

A 30× P/E with 30% growth has PEG = 1.0 (fair); a 15× P/E with 5% growth has PEG = 3.0 (actually expensive *for its growth*). PEG directly *defuses the SBI/Kotak trap*: SBI at 8× growing ~9% has PEG ≈ 0.9, while Kotak at 22× growing ~17% has PEG ≈ 1.3 — much closer than the raw 8×-vs-22× gap suggested. Once you adjust for growth, most of the "cheapness" of the low-P/E bank evaporates.

**PEG's own caveat:** it treats all growth as equal, ignoring *risk* and *sustainability* — a high-quality, predictable grower deserves a higher PEG than a fragile one. So PEG is a useful adjustment, not a final answer.

**🧠 Remember:** P/E = rupees paid per rupee of annual profit — great for stable, profitable companies but blind to growth and broken for loss-makers and cyclicals; PEG (P/E ÷ growth) fixes the growth blindness, with PEG ≈ 1 as fair value, though it still ignores risk and quality.

---

### CARD 3

> 💡 **CONCEPT: EV/EBITDA and EV/EBIT — the operating multiples, and when depreciation matters.**

**EV/EBITDA = Enterprise Value ÷ EBITDA.**

**What it measures:** how many rupees of *total firm value* you pay per rupee of core operating earnings (before interest, tax, depreciation, and amortisation). It's the price tag on the whole business's operating profitability, *independent of how it's financed* (recall the capital-structure neutrality from Session 12.7).

**When to use it:** comparing companies with **different capital structures** (its key strength); capital-intensive sectors; M&A analysis (acquirers think in EV terms); and companies with different tax situations. It's the sell-side's default multiple.

**When it lies (failure modes):** (1) **Banks and financials** — EBITDA is meaningless when interest *is* the core business (use P/B). (2) **High-capex businesses** — EBITDA flatters companies that must constantly spend to survive, because it ignores the capex needed to sustain those earnings. Charlie Munger famously dismissed EBITDA as "bullshit earnings" for exactly this reason: a company with gorgeous EBITDA but huge capex has poor *free* cash flow (the lesson from Session 12.3).

**Indian range:** large-caps ~**10–18×**; quality FMCG ~**25–40×**; cyclicals ~**5–9×**.

**Enter EV/EBIT — the depreciation-heavy fix.** EBIT = EBITDA − Depreciation & Amortisation. For most asset-light businesses, the difference is small. But for **depreciation-heavy sectors — telecom, infrastructure, cement, utilities — D&A is a massive, *real* economic cost.** Those network towers, fibre, plants, and turbines genuinely wear out and must be replaced. EV/EBITDA *flatters* such companies by pretending depreciation isn't a real expense; **EV/EBIT puts the depreciation back in, giving a truer picture of operating profitability.**

Take **Bharti Airtel**: its telecom network generates enormous depreciation. On EV/EBITDA, the company can look reasonably valued — but a huge chunk of that EBITDA gets consumed by depreciation as equipment ages and must be renewed. EV/EBIT reveals how much operating profit is *actually* left after accounting for the wearing-out of the network. So the rule: **for high-D&A, capital-intensive businesses, EV/EBIT (or a full DCF) is more honest than EV/EBITDA.** Using EV/EBITDA on a telecom or infra company systematically overstates how cheap it is.

**🧠 Remember:** EV/EBITDA prices the whole business's operating earnings neutral to capital structure (the sell-side default), but it flatters high-capex and high-depreciation businesses — so for depreciation-heavy sectors like telecom, infra, and cement, use EV/EBIT, which puts the real cost of wearing-out assets back in.

---

### CARD 4

> 🌍 **REAL WORLD: P/B — why banks live on it, and asset-light firms ignore it.**

**P/B (Price-to-Book) = Price ÷ Book Value per share = Market Cap ÷ Net Worth (shareholders' equity).**

**What it measures:** how many rupees you pay per rupee of *net assets* on the balance sheet. A P/B of 3× means you pay ₹3 for ₹1 of accounting net worth.

**Why P/B is the PRIMARY metric for banks.** A bank's assets *are* financial — loans, securities, cash — carried at close to fair value, so its book value is *economically meaningful* (unlike a factory booked at historical cost). And a bank's profitability is naturally a *return on that book* (ROE). The two are tightly linked: a bank's justified P/B rises with its sustainable ROE — high-ROE banks deserve high P/B, low-ROE banks low P/B. So for banks, you anchor on P/B and ROE together, not P/E or EV/EBITDA.

**The worked example — HDFC Bank vs SBI** *(figures approximate)*:

| Bank (NSE) | P/B (×) | ROE (~) |
|---|---|---|
| HDFC Bank (HDFCBANK) | ~2.7 | ~16–17% |
| State Bank of India (SBIN) | ~1.3 | ~16–18% |

Here's the fascinating part: in recent years SBI's ROE has *caught up to — even exceeded —* HDFC Bank's. Yet HDFC Bank still trades at roughly **double** SBI's P/B. Why, if their returns are similar? Because P/B prices not just the *level* of ROE but its *quality, consistency, and durability*: HDFC Bank's franchise — a low-cost CASA deposit base, clean asset quality, steady growth, strong governance — makes its returns more *trustworthy and sustainable*. SBI's lower P/B reflects PSU status, historically lumpier asset quality, government ownership, and less certain growth. **Same ROE, very different P/B = the market pricing the quality and risk of the returns, not just their size.** A profound lesson hiding in two numbers.

**When to ignore P/B (the failure mode):** **asset-light businesses** — IT, consumer, services. Their value lives in brands, people, and IP that *aren't on the balance sheet*, so book value is tiny and irrelevant. A software firm might show a P/B of 15× simply because its real assets are invisible to accounting — using P/B there is meaningless. For asset-light companies, use P/E or EV/EBITDA, never P/B.

**Indian range:** PSU banks ~**0.8–1.5×**; top private banks ~**2.5–3.5×**; asset-heavy industrials ~**1–3×**; asset-light — don't use.

**🧠 Remember:** P/B (price per rupee of net assets) is the primary multiple for banks because their book is real and ROE-driven — and HDFC Bank trading at ~2× SBI's P/B despite similar ROE shows P/B prices the *quality and durability* of returns; but P/B is useless for asset-light firms whose value isn't on the balance sheet.

---

### CARD 5

> 💡 **CONCEPT: EV/Sales — the multiple for companies that don't make money yet.**

**EV/Sales = Enterprise Value ÷ Revenue.**

**What it measures:** how many rupees of firm value you pay per rupee of *annual revenue*. It is the bluntest of all multiples — it completely ignores profitability and looks only at the top line.

**Why it exists — the loss-maker problem.** P/E needs positive earnings. EV/EBITDA needs positive EBITDA. But what about a company that's still *losing money*? Both of those multiples turn negative and meaningless. Yet the company still has *revenue* — so EV/Sales becomes the only workable multiple. That's exactly why **Zomato was valued on EV/Sales (and EV/GOV — gross order value) at its IPO**, not P/E: it was loss-making, so earnings-based multiples were useless. Recall from Session 12.1 that the market paid roughly **30× EV/Sales** for Zomato at listing — an enormous revenue multiple reflecting a bet on future dominance and eventual profitability.

**When to use it:** (1) **loss-making companies** (the primary case); (2) early-stage, high-growth businesses pre-profitability; (3) companies with *temporarily* depressed or negative margins (a cyclical trough); (4) comparing businesses where you expect margins to *converge* over time.

**When it lies (the failure mode):** because it ignores profitability entirely, EV/Sales treats a company with fat margins and one with razor-thin margins *identically* if their revenue is the same — which is absurd. A high EV/Sales is only justified if high (future) margins justify it. So you can *only* compare EV/Sales across companies with *similar or convergent* margin profiles. Comparing the EV/Sales of a high-margin software firm to a low-margin trading company is meaningless. The implicit question behind every EV/Sales multiple is: *"what margin does this revenue eventually earn?"* — and if you can't answer that, the multiple tells you little.

**Indian range:** wildly margin-dependent — low-margin businesses ~**0.5–2× sales**; high-margin or high-growth ~**5–15×+**; loss-making platforms on a strong growth story can command very high multiples on optionality alone (Zomato's ~30× being a vivid example).

**🧠 Remember:** EV/Sales (firm value per rupee of revenue) is the go-to multiple for loss-making and pre-profit companies like Zomato at IPO (~30×) where earnings multiples fail — but because it ignores profitability, it only works comparing businesses with similar or convergent margins, and a high EV/Sales is only justified by high future margins.

---

### CARD 6

> 📊 **VISUAL: The Multiple Matchmaker — which tool for which business (and two specialists).**

*[Plain English description — what the student should imagine]*

There is no universal multiple — each fits a specific kind of business. Picture a decision matrix that maps a company's profile to the right tool, so you never again reach for P/E on a loss-maker or P/B on a software firm:

| Company profile | Best multiple | Why |
|---|---|---|
| Profitable, stable, asset-light (FMCG, IT, pharma) | **P/E** | Clean earnings; compare on price per rupee of profit |
| High-growth, profitable | **PEG** | Adjusts P/E for the growth you're paying for |
| Different capital structures / capital-intensive | **EV/EBITDA** | Neutral to debt; operating focus |
| Depreciation-heavy (telecom, infra, cement) | **EV/EBIT** | Puts the real cost of wearing-out assets back in |
| Banks, NBFCs, financials, asset-heavy | **P/B** (with ROE) | Book value is real and ROE-driven |
| Loss-making / pre-profit (platforms, startups) | **EV/Sales** | Only positive metric available |
| Mature, high-payout (PSUs, utilities) | **Dividend Yield** | Return comes largely from dividends |
| Capital-allocation / value-creation focus | **EV/Invested Capital** (with ROIC) | Tests whether the multiple is justified by returns |

**Two specialists worth knowing.**

**Dividend Yield = Dividend per share ÷ Price.** The cash return you earn from dividends alone — a 6% yield means ₹6 of dividend per ₹100 invested. Use it for **mature, stable, high-payout businesses** where growth is limited and the return comes mostly from income — **PSUs (Coal India, Power Grid, ONGC), utilities, mature FMCG.** **Coal India**, for instance, has at times yielded ~8–10%, compensating investors for its low growth with fat dividends (the flip side of its low P/E from Session 12.1). Indian range: PSUs/utilities ~4–10%; mature companies ~1–3%; growth companies ~0–1%.

**EV/Invested Capital (with ROIC).** How many rupees of firm value you pay per rupee of capital actually *invested* in the business. Its power is the pairing with ROIC: a value-*creating* company (ROIC > WACC) deserves EV/IC > 1; a value-*destroyer* (ROIC < WACC) deserves EV/IC < 1. So EV/IC tells you whether the market is rationally rewarding value creation — the multiple version of the ROCE-vs-WACC test from Session 12.4. Niche, but conceptually the deepest.

**AI Image Generation Prompt:**
> Create a widescreen 16:9 decision-matrix infographic, dark cosmic aesthetic — deep navy background (#02073D) with a faint starfield. Title in clean white sans-serif: "THE MULTIPLE MATCHMAKER — WHICH TOOL FOR WHICH BUSINESS". Render a clean two-column table with 8 rows. Left column "COMPANY PROFILE" (in #F5ECFF lavender), right column "BEST MULTIPLE" (the multiple name in bold #246BFD blue, with a one-line reason beneath in small grey). Rows: "Profitable, stable, asset-light → P/E"; "High-growth, profitable → PEG"; "Different capital structures → EV/EBITDA"; "Depreciation-heavy (telecom, infra) → EV/EBIT"; "Banks & financials → P/B (with ROE)"; "Loss-making / pre-profit → EV/Sales"; "Mature, high-payout (PSUs) → Dividend Yield"; "Value-creation focus → EV/Invested Capital". Use a #FF6AC1 pink accent line separating header from rows. Bottom caption in #6A5CFF purple: "No universal multiple — match the tool to the business." Style: clean, modern, high-contrast, generous row spacing, Bricolage-Grotesque-style headings, monospace for the multiple names.

**Why this visual works:** It converts eight separate tools into a single at-a-glance lookup, so the student internalises the *matching* skill — the actual point of the session — rather than memorising eight definitions in isolation. It's the kind of reference a junior analyst pins above their desk.

**🧠 Remember:** There's no universal multiple — match the tool to the business: P/E for stable earners, PEG for growth, EV/EBITDA for varied capital structures, EV/EBIT for depreciation-heavy firms, P/B for banks, EV/Sales for loss-makers, Dividend Yield for mature payers, and EV/Invested Capital for value-creation analysis.

---

### CARD 7

> ⚠️ **THE MISTAKE: Using the right multiple on the wrong business.**

The single most common multiples error isn't a calculation slip — it's a *matching* failure: applying a multiple in a situation where it structurally breaks down. Each multiple has a domain where it works and a domain where it actively lies, and the analyst who confuses them looks foolish to anyone who knows better.

**Why it's so easy to make:** multiples feel universal — "P/E is P/E," so why not use it everywhere? But each one rests on hidden assumptions (positive earnings, comparable capital intensity, meaningful book value) that quietly fail for certain businesses, and the multiple gives you a confident number anyway.

**The classic failure modes — memorise these:**
- **P/E on a loss-making company** → negative and meaningless. *Fix:* EV/Sales.
- **P/E on a cyclical at peak earnings** → inflated profits make the P/E look deceptively *low*, a textbook value trap. A steel or commodity stock at a 5× P/E during a boom is often *most* dangerous, not cheapest. *Fix:* normalise earnings to mid-cycle, or use replacement-cost/EV-per-tonne.
- **EV/EBITDA on a bank** → EBITDA is meaningless when interest is the core business. *Fix:* P/B with ROE.
- **P/B on an asset-light company** → book value is trivial, so P/B looks absurdly high and tells you nothing. *Fix:* P/E or EV/EBITDA.
- **EV/EBITDA on a high-depreciation telecom or infra company** → flatters by ignoring the real cost of wearing-out assets. *Fix:* EV/EBIT or a full DCF.
- **Comparing any multiple across different growth/risk without adjusting** → the P/E trap (SBI vs Kotak). *Fix:* adjust for growth (PEG), returns, and risk.

**The unifying lesson:** before applying any multiple, ask *"does this multiple's hidden assumption hold for this business?"* A profitable, stable, asset-light company can take a P/E. A bank needs P/B. A loss-maker needs EV/Sales. A cyclical needs normalised earnings. The multiple is a tool, and like any tool it's superb for its job and useless — even dangerous — for the wrong one.

**What the interviewer thinks:** a candidate who says "this steel stock is at 5× earnings, so it's cheap" (at a cyclical peak) signals *"doesn't understand cyclicality — will buy the top."* A candidate who says "5× looks cheap, but I'd check whether these are peak-cycle earnings before trusting it" signals real judgment. The cyclical-P/E trap, in particular, is a favourite interview test.

**🧠 Remember:** The biggest multiples mistake is matching, not maths — P/E breaks on loss-makers and cyclical peaks, EV/EBITDA breaks on banks and high-depreciation firms, P/B breaks on asset-light businesses — so always check whether a multiple's hidden assumption holds before applying it.

---

### CARD 8

> 📝 **SESSION SUMMARY: What You Now Know**

```
🎯 The Big Idea:
Every multiple is a story with a specific job — and a specific way of lying. There's
no universal multiple; the skill is matching the right tool to the business and knowing
each tool's failure mode. A multiple is a question ("why this price?"), never a verdict.

✅ 5 Things You Now Know:
1. P/E (price per rupee of profit) suits stable, profitable, asset-light firms but is
   blind to growth (fix: PEG = P/E ÷ growth, ≈1 = fair) and breaks on loss-makers and
   cyclical peaks. SBI's 8× isn't "cheaper" than Kotak's 22× — it's a different story.
2. EV/EBITDA prices operating earnings neutral to capital structure (sell-side default),
   but flatters high-capex/high-depreciation firms — use EV/EBIT for telecom, infra, cement.
3. P/B (price per rupee of net assets) is the PRIMARY bank multiple (book is real,
   ROE-driven) — HDFC at ~2× SBI's P/B despite similar ROE shows it prices return QUALITY.
   Useless for asset-light firms.
4. EV/Sales is for loss-makers (Zomato ~30× at IPO) where earnings multiples fail — but
   it ignores profitability, so only compare businesses with convergent margins.
5. Specialists: Dividend Yield for mature high-payers (Coal India ~8–10%); EV/Invested
   Capital (with ROIC) to test whether a multiple is justified by value creation.

⚡ The Analyst Move:
A textbook student applies P/E to everything. A working analyst matches the multiple to
the business, checks the multiple's hidden assumption holds, adjusts for growth and risk
before comparing, and treats a low multiple as a question ("is the market right to be
cautious?") rather than an automatic buy signal.

🔗 Connects to:
• Session 12.1 — a multiple is a story; "low P/E" ≠ cheap.
• Session 12.7 — EV vs equity multiples and the discipline of comparison.
• Session 12.9 — sector-specific valuation: why banks, insurers, IT, FMCG, and cyclicals
  each demand their own framework (deep dive on the bank/financial multiples).

💬 If someone asks you "which multiple would you use to value this company?" at an interview, say:
"It depends entirely on the business. For a stable, profitable, asset-light company I'd use
P/E, and PEG if growth is a big part of the story. For companies with different capital
structures or capital intensity I'd use EV/EBITDA — or EV/EBIT if depreciation is large,
like telecom or infrastructure. For a bank I'd use Price-to-Book alongside ROE, because the
book value is real and returns are ROE-driven. For a loss-making company I'd use EV/Sales,
since earnings multiples don't work. And for mature, high-payout names like PSUs, dividend
yield matters. The key is matching the tool to the business — and never reading a low
multiple as automatically cheap without checking growth, returns, and risk."
```

## QUIZ (5 QUESTIONS)

---

**Q1. Which multiple is most appropriate for valuing a loss-making company like Zomato at its IPO?**

A) P/E (Price-to-Earnings)
B) EV/Sales
C) Dividend Yield
D) EV/EBITDA

**✅ Answer: B**

**Explanation:** A loss-making company has negative earnings and often negative EBITDA, so P/E and EV/EBITDA turn meaningless — but it still has revenue, so EV/Sales (or EV/GOV) is the workable multiple, which is exactly why Zomato was valued on it (~30×) at listing. Dividend Yield (C) is irrelevant for a loss-maker that pays no dividend. The trade-off is that EV/Sales ignores profitability, so it only works when you have a view on future margins.

**Difficulty:** Easy

---

**Q2. Why should a depreciation-heavy telecom company like Bharti Airtel generally be compared on EV/EBIT rather than EV/EBITDA?**

A) Because EBIT is always larger than EBITDA
B) Because EV/EBITDA ignores the very large, real cost of depreciation, which flatters capital-intensive businesses, while EV/EBIT puts that cost back in
C) Because telecom companies have no debt
D) Because EBITDA cannot be calculated for telecom companies

**✅ Answer: B**

**Explanation:** For telecom, infrastructure, and similar sectors, depreciation is a massive *real* economic cost — network equipment genuinely wears out and must be replaced — so EV/EBITDA overstates how cheap the company is by pretending depreciation isn't an expense. EV/EBIT (EBITDA minus D&A) reflects that cost and gives a truer operating picture. Option A is false (EBIT is *smaller* than EBITDA), C is false (telecoms are typically debt-heavy), and D is false (EBITDA is computable, just misleading here).

**Difficulty:** Medium

---

**Q3. HDFC Bank trades at roughly 2.7× book value while SBI trades at about 1.3×, even though their ROEs have recently been similar. What best explains the gap?**

A) HDFC Bank is clearly overvalued and should be sold
B) P/B prices the quality, consistency, and durability of returns — not just their level — and HDFC Bank's cleaner franchise, asset quality, and governance make its returns more trustworthy
C) SBI has no profits
D) The two banks are in different industries

**✅ Answer: B**

**Explanation:** Two banks can earn similar ROEs yet command very different P/B multiples, because the market pays for the *quality* of those returns — HDFC Bank's low-cost deposit franchise, clean asset quality, steady growth, and strong governance make its ROE more durable and predictable than a PSU bank's. SBI's lower P/B reflects PSU status, lumpier asset quality, and government overhang. Option A is an unsupported leap, C is false (SBI is highly profitable), and D is wrong (both are banks).

**Difficulty:** Medium

---

**Q4. A company has a P/E of 30× and expected earnings growth of 20% per year. What is its PEG ratio, and what does it suggest?**

A) 1.5 — somewhat expensive relative to its growth
B) 0.67 — undervalued
C) 1.5 — undervalued
D) 600 — wildly overvalued

**✅ Answer: A**

**Explanation:** PEG = P/E ÷ growth rate = 30 ÷ 20 = 1.5. A PEG above 1 suggests the stock is somewhat expensive *relative to its growth* (Peter Lynch's benchmark for fair value is roughly 1.0). Option B inverts the ratio (20 ÷ 30), C has the right number but the wrong interpretation (1.5 is *not* undervalued), and D multiplies instead of divides. Caveat: PEG ignores risk and the sustainability of growth, so a high-quality predictable grower can justify a PEG above 1.

**Difficulty:** Medium

---

**Q5. A cyclical steel company is trading at a P/E of just 5× during a commodity boom. What is the most likely correct interpretation?**

A) It's extremely cheap — buy it immediately
B) The low P/E may be a value trap, because peak-cycle earnings inflate the denominator and make the stock look cheap right before profits fall
C) The company is about to go bankrupt
D) A 5× P/E always means a stock is undervalued

**✅ Answer: B**

**Explanation:** For cyclicals, a low P/E at the top of the cycle is a classic trap: boom-time earnings are unsustainably high, so the P/E looks low precisely when profits are about to mean-revert downward — buying then often means buying the peak. The disciplined response is to normalise earnings to mid-cycle (or use replacement-cost / EV-per-tonne) rather than trust the headline multiple. Option A is the trap itself, C is an unsupported jump to insolvency, and D is the dangerous oversimplification this session dismantles — a low P/E is never *automatically* cheap.

**Difficulty:** Hard

---
---

## INTERVIEW QUESTIONS (3 QUESTIONS)

---

**IQ1. "How do you decide which valuation multiple to use for a given company?"**

*Difficulty:* Senior Analyst
*Type:* Judgment
*Firm type likely to ask:* All

**💬 Strong answer:**
"It depends entirely on the business, because each multiple has a specific job and a specific failure mode. For a profitable, stable, asset-light company — say an FMCG or IT firm — I'd use P/E, and I'd look at PEG if growth is a big part of the thesis, since P/E alone ignores growth. For companies with different capital structures or high capital intensity, I'd use EV/EBITDA because it's neutral to leverage — but if depreciation is very large, like telecom or infrastructure, I'd switch to EV/EBIT so the real cost of the assets wearing out is captured. For banks and financials, I'd use Price-to-Book alongside ROE, because their book value is genuinely meaningful and returns are ROE-driven. For a loss-making company I'd use EV/Sales, since earnings multiples don't work. And for mature, high-payout names like PSUs or utilities, dividend yield matters. The overarching principle is to match the tool to the business and check that the multiple's hidden assumption — positive earnings, comparable capital intensity, meaningful book value — actually holds before I trust it."

**🚫 Weak answer trap:** Saying "I'd use P/E" as a default for everything, or not being able to name the right tool for a bank or a loss-maker — it signals you don't understand that multiples are situational.

**✅ What this question tests:** Whether you can match the multiple to the business and articulate each one's domain and failure mode — the core skill of relative valuation, and a guaranteed interview question.

---

**IQ2. "Why are banks valued on Price-to-Book rather than EV/EBITDA?"**

*Difficulty:* Junior Analyst
*Type:* Technical
*Firm type likely to ask:* All

**💬 Strong answer:**
"Two reasons. First, EV/EBITDA simply doesn't work for a bank, because for a bank interest income and interest expense *are* the core operating business — you can't strip out financing the way you do for an industrial company, so EBITDA is meaningless. Second, Price-to-Book genuinely fits banks: their assets are financial — loans and securities carried close to fair value — so book value is economically real, unlike a factory booked at historical cost. And a bank's profitability is naturally a return on that book, which is ROE, so P/B and ROE go hand in hand — a bank's justified P/B rises with its sustainable ROE. That's why you'll always see banks compared on P/B with ROE, with high-quality franchises like HDFC Bank commanding a premium P/B even over PSU banks with similar reported ROE, because the market is pricing the durability and quality of those returns, not just their level."

**🚫 Weak answer trap:** Saying "that's just the convention" without explaining *why* — that EBITDA is meaningless for banks and book value is real and ROE-linked.

**✅ What this question tests:** Whether you understand what makes a bank fundamentally different — that financing is its business, so book value and ROE, not operating multiples, are the right lens. A staple of any financials interview.

---

**IQ3. "A cyclical company is trading at a P/E of 5×. Is it cheap?"**

*Difficulty:* Senior Analyst
*Type:* Judgment
*Firm type likely to ask:* Buy-side / Sell-side

**💬 Strong answer:**
"Not necessarily — and for a cyclical, a very low P/E is often a warning rather than a bargain. The first thing I'd check is *where in the cycle* the company is. If it's at a cyclical peak, like a steel or commodity producer during a boom, its earnings are temporarily inflated, which deflates the P/E and makes the stock look cheap right before profits mean-revert downward. That's the classic value trap — the lowest P/E often appears at the most dangerous moment. So I wouldn't trust the headline multiple. I'd normalise the earnings to mid-cycle margins to see what the company earns through the cycle, not just at the top, and I might cross-check with an asset-based measure like EV-per-tonne or replacement cost, which is more stable for cyclicals than earnings. If, after normalising, it still looks cheap, then it might genuinely be — but a 5× P/E at a peak is far more likely to be a trap than a gift. Conversely, a high P/E at a cyclical *trough*, when earnings are depressed, can actually signal value."

**🚫 Weak answer trap:** "Yes, 5× is cheap, buy it" — revealing no understanding of cyclicality and the peak-earnings trap, which is exactly what this question is designed to expose.

**✅ What this question tests:** Whether you understand that P/E lies for cyclicals and know to normalise earnings across the cycle — a favourite judgment question precisely because it catches people who read multiples mechanically.

---

*End of Session 12.8 — Multiples Decoded: P/E, EV/EBITDA, P/B, EV/Sales, and the Rest*
